\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
and
\Gamma/\mu\vdash A
then there is a map
\mathsf{coe}^{\mu\leq\nu}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\nu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
.

Proof.

Though this is an elementary result, we give a proof to highlight the process of working in

MTT
. Suppose we are given
x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
, we must construct a term of type

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\nu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
. Using the elimination principle for
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
, we may fix

x_{0}\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A
and assume that
x=\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. Next, using the introduction rule for

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\nu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
, it suffices to construct an element of
A
, though we must update the
annotations on
x
and
x_{0}
to
x_{0}\mathrel{:_{\mu/\nu}}A
and

x\mathrel{:_{\mathsf{id}_{/}\nu}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
. Finally, we may use the variable rule to access
x_{0}:A

as required. All told then, the full term is given as follows:

\mathsf{coe}^{\mu\leq\nu}=\lambda x.\,\mathsf{let}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\leftarrow x\ \mathsf{in}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\nu}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

This pattern of binding a variable of type
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
and immediately pattern-matching upon it
is exceptionally common. Accordingly, we adopt the following “pattern-matching” style notation
for convenience:

\mathsf{coe}^{\mu\leq\nu}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\qed

{notation}

We shall also have occasion to use the convenience feature of modalized dependent products

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}
which abstract over
a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A
directly rather
than
a\mathrel{:_{\mathsf{id}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
to allow us to avoid immediately pattern-matching on
a
.
In particular,
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is equivalent to

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a\mathrel{:_{\mathsf{id}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{let}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\leftarrow a\ \mathsf{in}\ B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. We shall most often
use this when working informally to type theory. For instance, if we write “given

a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A
, there exists an element of
B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}
” this should be interpreted as
denoting the type
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

3.
Triangulated type theory

As already mentioned, part of our strategy is to replicate the argument of
WL (20)
in
STT
combined with

MTT
and build a directed univalent universe of groupoids. However, there is a fundamental problem
with this approach:
WL (20)
rely on a particular modality (the right adjoint to
\mathbb{I}\to-
) in their construction, and the standard model of simplicial type theory in
\mathrm{PSh}(\mathbbl{\Delta})

simply does not have an analog to this modality. Thus, we need more than a combination of
STT

and
MTT
, we need a new system which admits a model where
\mathbb{I}\to-
is a left adjoint.

To motivate our solution, let us further consider the intended model of
STT
in simplicial spaces
for a moment. Up to the complexity needed to model homotopy type theory, these are simplicial sets
i.e., presheaves on
\mathbbl{\Delta}
, the category of finite, inhabited total linear orders. The interval

\mathbb{I}
is realized by
\mathbf{y}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{]}})
. Our problem then amounts to the fact that exponentiation by

\mathbf{y}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{]}})
does not have a right adjoint.
7
7

7

This is easiest to check by observing that it
does not commute with pushouts.
However, there is a category closely related to
\mathbbl{\Delta}
which has
also received a great deal of attention by type theorists interested in cubical type theory: the
category of (Dedekind) cubes
\square
, the full subcategory of partial orders spanned by
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0\leq 1$}\mathclose{}{\}}}^{n}
for all
n
. The category
\square
enjoys two properties which make it interesting for simplicial type
theorists: there is a fully faithful functor
\mathrm{PSh}(\mathbbl{\Delta})\to\mathrm{PSh}(\square)
which preserves the
interval and admits both left and right
adjoints
(
Sat, 19
;
KV, 20
;
SW, 21
)
, and within
\mathrm{PSh}(\square)

exponentiating by the interval is a left adjoint.

Accordingly, we introduce a relaxation of simplicial type theory intended to capture (the
homotopical version of)
\mathrm{PSh}(\square)
. Within this type theory, we can recover simplicial type
theory by studying those types which are in the image of the aforementioned embedding alongside the
amazing right adjoint necessary for constructing our sought-after universe
(
Lic+, 18
)
.

Concretely, we work within a version of
MTT
instantiated with several modalities, further
extended by a bounded distributive lattice
\mathbb{I}:\mathsf{HSet}
which serves as our weakened version of
the interval, and a handful of axioms. Notably, we no longer assume that
\mathbb{I}
is totally ordered
and instead ask for it to be a bounded distributive lattice; this is our central deviation from
simplicial type theory.

In this section, we introduce
triangulated type theory
as an extension of
MTT
. For
precision, we give a complete listing of the axioms we require (including univalence, the existence
of an interval, etc.). Finally, we explore a few elementary consequences of this axiom and produce
our first non-trivial examples of categories.

3.1.
The definition of triangulated type theory

We begin by describing the particular instantiation of
MTT
needed for
TT
\boxbslash
.

3.1.1.
The mode theory

As mentioned in
Section

2.2
,
MTT
must be instantiated by a mode theory. In our case, we
shall require only one mode
m
which we shall think of as cubical spaces
\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{sSet}}(\square)
. We
shall then add the following modalities

•

A pair of modalities
\flat,\sharp
internalizing the global sections comonad and its right adjoint.

•

A modality
\mathrm{op}
internalizing the “opposite.”

Intuitively,
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid X$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
is a type with the same points as
X
, but with all the higher cubes
reversed; if there was a line joining
x_{0}
to
x_{1}
in
X
, then
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid X$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
will have a line
joining
x_{1}
to
x_{0}
instead.

On the other hand,
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
deletes all (higher) cells from a type, leaving only the
underlying groupoid of points (its
groupoid core
). We shall use this modality to define the
core of a category and, more generally, use it to isolate discrete categories. Owing to this second
point, prior work has often referred to
\flat
as the
discrete

modality
(
Shu, 18
;
MR, 23
)
. Its right adjoint,
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
, is slightly less
intuitive. Operationally, it deletes all higher cells from a type and then adds in a unique (higher)
cell between every collection of points. We caution the reader that while
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid X$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
is
always a groupoid in the sense of
Section

2
, it is often the case that
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp\mid X$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
is
not a category even if
X
was originally a category. This is not unexpected:
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}

models the core functor sending a category to its underlying groupoid, but this operation when
restricted to categories has no right adjoint. It is only in a bigger category such as cubical or simplicial spaces
that
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
exists.

We require a number of equations and inequalities to force these modalities to behave as expected.
In particular, we require the following 2-cells and equations on modalities:

\flat\circ\flat=\flat\circ\sharp=\flat\circ\mathrm{op}=\mathrm{op}\circ\flat=\flat\sharp\circ\sharp=\sharp\circ\flat=\sharp\circ\mathrm{op}=\mathrm{op}\circ\sharp=\sharp\\
\mathrm{op}\circ\mathrm{op}=\mathsf{id}\flat\leq\mathsf{id}\mathsf{id}\leq\sharp

We refer to the 2-category theory generated by these constraints as
\mathcal{M}_{\boxbslash}
.

3.1.2.
The interval

As mentioned previously, we require an interval in order to capture the simplicial (or, in our case,
cubical) structure.

{axiom}

[The interval]

There exists a bounded distributive lattice
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbb{I}:\mathsf{HSet},\land,\lor,0,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

Our next axiom controls the behavior of the opposite modality on
\mathbb{I}
:

{axiom}
[Opposite of
\mathbb{I}
]

There is an equivalence
\neg:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to\mathbb{I}
which swaps
0
for
1
and
\lor
for
\land
.

{notation}

In various places, it will be convenient to treat
\neg
as a function
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathbb{I}
to
avoid spuriously introducing
\mathsf{mod}_{\mathrm{op}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. The two types
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathbb{I}
and

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to\mathbb{I}
are canonically equivalent and so this causes no ambiguity, see
Section

2.2
.

With
\mathbb{I}
, we are now able to postulate an amazing right adjoint operation to
\mathbb{I}\to-
. As was
noted in
Lic+ (18)
, this operation cannot be defined as a map
\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}
.
Accordingly, we restrict its action to global elements using
\flat
.

{axiom}

[
\mathbb{I}
is tiny]

The following proposition holds:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A_{\mathbb{I}}\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}}\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{\epsilon\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A_{\mathbb{I}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{\mathbb{I}}\to A}\textstyle\prod\nolimits_{B\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}}\mathsf{isEquiv}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid B\to A_{\mathbb{I}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid B^{\mathbb{I}}\to A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

In other words, we require that for each element
A\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}
, there exists a type

A_{\mathbb{I}}
which represents the functor
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{\mathbb{I}}\to A
i.e., a right adjoint to
\mathbb{I}\to-
.

3.1.3.
The simplicial monad

Before moving on to the list of additional axioms that form
TT
\boxbslash
, we must take a moment to discuss
an additional construct: the simplicial monad. As motivation, while we have already noted that the
interval is not totally ordered, there is a large number of types which “act as though it
is.” The simplicial monad isolates and classifies these types.

More precisely, a type is
simplicial
if it satisfies the following predicate:

\mathsf{isSimp}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i\,j:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{isEquiv}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\lambda a\,z.\,a:A\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

If a type
A
satisfies
\mathsf{isSimp}
, this acts as a license to totally order elements of the interval
whenever we are constructing an element of
A
. Furthermore, as the name suggests, simplicial types
are those which come from simplicial rather than cubical sets (see
Section

4
).

Proposition 3.1 (
RSS (20)
).

There is a monad
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\boxbslash:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U},\eta,\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}
such that:

•

For every
A:\mathcal{U}
,
\mathsf{isSimp}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\boxbslash A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
holds.

•

If
B
is simplicial, then
\eta^{*}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\boxbslash A\to B$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A\to B$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is an
equivalence.

•

\boxbslash
commutes with dependent sums and the identity type.

We refer to
\boxbslash
as the
simplicial monad
8
8

8

The notation
\boxbslash
is chosen
deliberately: simplicial types are those which believe the square
\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}
(along with
all hypercubes) comes from gluing together a pair of triangles

\Delta^{2}\mathrel{\textstyle\sqcup_{\mathbb{I}}}\Delta^{2}
.
and write
\mathcal{U}_{\boxbslash}
for the subtype

\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:\mathcal{U}}\mathsf{isSimp}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

{convention}

We reserve the words “category” and “groupoid” for types which are simplicial in addition to
satisfying the Segal/Rezk conditions from
STT
. Accordingly, e.g.
category
signifies a
type which is simplicial, Segal, and Rezk complete.

3.1.4.
Additional axioms

Finally, we require a handful of additional axioms which either improve the behavior of modalities
generally or form a more tight correspondence between our system and our intended model. We offer
some intuition for each axiom and note that each is validated by the intended model described in

Section

4
.

Our first two axioms are general and common assumptions in univalent modal type theory.
First, we record the univalence axiom here as previously discussed in
Section

2
:

{axiom}
[Univalence]

We assume that each universe
\mathcal{U}_{i}
is univalent.

Next, we assume that each modality
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
commutes with identity types.

{axiom}
[Crisp induction]

For every
\mu
, the canonical map
\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle b$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu\mid a=b$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
is an
equivalence.

{remark}

It is open whether
Section

3.1.4
implies
Section

3.1.4
. However, in all sensible models,

Section

3.1.4
does hold and its failure to do so is more indicative of the poor
behavior of the intensional identity type than anything else.

After these fairly general reasoning principles, we now have a sequence of more
simplicial-specific axioms. The first of these links the global sections modality to the
interval. In particular, it states that the global sections of a type always form a groupoid.

{axiom}
[
\mathbb{I}
detects discreteness]

If
A\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}
then
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to A
is an equivalence (
A
is

discrete
) if and only if
A\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbb{I}\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is an equivalence (
A
is
\mathbb{I}
-null).

The next axiom states that the global points of
\mathbb{I}
itself are just
0
and
1
and that
0\neq 1
:

{axiom}
[Global points of
\mathbb{I}
]

The canonical map
{\mathsf{Bool}}\to\mathbb{I}
is injective and
{\mathsf{Bool}}\simeq\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
.

In our intended model, various properties can be proven by “testing” them at the representable
presheaves
\mathbf{y}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0\leq 1$}\mathclose{}{\}}}^{n})
. We include a version of this idea as an axiom in our
theory. Namely, we assert that maps between global types can be tested for invertibility at

\mathbb{I}^{n}
:

{axiom}
[Cubes separate]

A map
f\mathrel{:_{\flat}}A\to B
is an equivalence if and only if the following holds:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle n\mathrel{:_{\flat}}{\mathsf{Nat}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{isEquiv}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f_{*}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\mathbb{I}^{n}\to A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\mathbb{I}^{n}\to B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

This follows from another possible axiom,
cubes detect continuity
,
following
MR (23)
. Note that if
A
and
B
are simplicial, one can derive a version of

Section

3.1.4
which replaces
\mathbb{I}^{n}
with
\Delta^{n}
.

It is relatively easy to characterize maps out of
\boxbslash A
as they are closely related to maps out
of
A
itself. It is much harder, however, to characterize
X\to\boxbslash A
. Our next axiom states
that in certain favorable cases these, too, coincide with the corresponding situation for
A
:

{axiom}
[Simplicial stability]

If
A\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}
then the following map is an equivalence for all
n\mathrel{:_{\flat}}{\mathsf{Nat}}
:

\eta_{*}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\Delta^{n}\to A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\Delta^{n}\to\boxbslash A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}

Finally, while simplicial type theory allows us to prove many interesting facts about maps out of
the interval, it is far more difficult to prove properties about
X\to\mathbb{I}
. In order to balance
the scales, we follow
CCH (23)
and add a duality
axiom
(
Koc, 14
;
Ble, 23
)
characterizing these maps in certain special cases.
Prior to stating this principle, we require the following definition:

Definition 3.2.

A map
\mathbb{I}\to A
of bounded distributive lattices is a
finitely presented (fp)

\mathbb{I}
-algebra
if it is merely equivalent to the canonical map
\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{I}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}$}\mathclose{}{]}}/\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle t_{1}=s_{1},\dots,t_{m}=s_{m}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
for some
n,m
.

The definition of a homomorphism of bounded distributive lattices (a map which commutes with

0,1,\land,\lor
) extends to a notion of homomorphism between fp
\mathbb{I}
-algebras
\hom_{\mathbb{I}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,B$}\mathclose{}{)}}

by further requiring the underlying map to commute with the maps
\mathbb{I}\to A
and
\mathbb{I}\to B
.

{axiom}

[Duality]

Given an fp
\mathbb{I}
-algebra
f:\mathbb{I}\to A
the following map is an equivalence:

\lambda a\,g.\,g\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}:A\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\hom_{\mathbb{I}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\to\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

Definition 3.3.

Triangulated type theory
TT
\boxbslash
is
MTT
with mode theory
\mathcal{M}_{\boxbslash}
extended by

Sections

3.1.2
,
3.1.2
,
3.1.2
,
3.1.4
,
3.1.4
,
3.1.4
,
3.1.4
,
3.1.4
,
3.1.4
and
3.1.4
.

3.2.
Duality and
\Delta^{n}

Section

3.1.4
has a number of remarkable consequences for
\mathbb{I}
. While these are not specific to
directed univalent universes, they allow us to construct the first non-trivial categories inside

TT
\boxbslash
. We begin with the following result—independently proven by

PS (25)
—reminiscent of various principles from synthetic domain theory.

Lemma 3.4 (Phoa’s principle).

Evaluation at
0,1
is an embedding
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}
with image

\Delta^{2}
.

Proof.

We first will argue via
Section

3.1.4
that
\mathbb{I}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{]}}
is equivalent to
\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{I}
via the
evaluation map. To see this, let us note that
\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{I}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{]}}
is an
\mathbb{I}
-algebra by
definition, and
\hom_{\mathbb{I}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbb{I}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{]}},\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\simeq\mathbb{I}
. Accordingly, by
Section

3.1.4
, the
map
\mathsf{eval}:\mathbb{I}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{]}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is an equivalence.

By the 2-for-3 principle of equivalences, it then suffices to show that evaluating a polynomial at

0
and
1
induces an embedding
\mathbb{I}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{]}}\to\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}
whose image is
\Delta^{2}
. An
inductive argument allows us to conclude that
\mathsf{eval}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p,-$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is a monotone map from

\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{I}
and so evaluation of polynomials at endpoints factors through
\Delta^{2}
. We
therefore are reduced to showing that this map is an equivalence. To see this, we observe that
any polynomial in one variable can be placed in the following normal form:

p=\mathsf{eval}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}\lor x\land\mathsf{eval}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}
whereby the conclusion is immediate.
∎

{notation}

In light of the equivalence used in the proof of Phoa’s principle, we will no longer distinguish
between polynomials in one variable
\mathbb{I}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{]}}
and functions
\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{I}
.

Lemma 3.5 (Generalized Phoa’s principle).

•

The evaluation map from
\mathbb{I}^{n}\to\mathbb{I}
to monotone maps
{\mathsf{Bool}}^{n}\to\mathbb{I}
is an
equivalence.

•

The evaluation map from
\Delta^{n}\to\mathbb{I}
to monotone maps
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0\leq\dots\leq n$}\mathclose{}{]}}\to\mathbb{I}
is an
equivalence.

In the above, we have regarded
{\mathsf{Bool}}
as a 2-element partial order
{\mathsf{ff}}\leq{\mathsf{tt}}
.

Both claims follow from induction on
n
and repeated application of Phoa’s principle.

{remark}

The particular cube category used in our intended model of
TT
\boxbslash
is equivalent by Birkhoff
duality to the category of
flat
finite bounded distributive
lattices
(
Spi, 16
)
.
Lemma

3.5
is a manifestation of this
fact.

Lemma 3.6.

\mathbb{I}
is simplicial.

Proof.

To show that
\mathbb{I}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is an equivalence, it suffices,
by
Section

3.1.4
, to consider
f,g\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathbb{I}^{n}\to\mathbb{I}
and show that the
following is an equivalence:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\mathbb{I}^{n}\to\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\vec{x}:\mathbb{I}^{n}$}\mathrel{{|}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f(\vec{x})\leq g(\vec{x})\lor g(\vec{x})\leq f(\vec{x})$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\to\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}

Using
Lemma

3.5
, we can extend an element of the codomain to a total
function
\mathbb{I}^{n}\to\mathbb{I}
provided we can specify its behavior on
\vec{x}:{\mathsf{Bool}}^{n}
. The
proposition
f(\vec{x})\leq g(\vec{x})\lor g(\vec{x})\leq f(\vec{x})
holds for all
\vec{x}:{\mathsf{Bool}}^{n}
,
and so such an extension always exists and is necessarily unique.
∎

{remark}

A more elegant proof of the above was recently provided by
Wil (25)
.

Corollary 3.7.

\Delta^{n}
is a category.

Proof.

Since there are no nontrivial invertible morphisms in
\Delta^{n}
, it is trivially Rezk-complete and, as a
retract of
\mathbb{I}^{n}
, it is simplicial. Therefore, it suffices to show that
\Delta^{n}
is Segal.

To this end, let us consider
\Lambda^{2}_{1}\to\Delta^{n}
. This is equivalent to a pair of maps

f,g:\mathbb{I}\to\Delta^{n}
such that
f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}=g\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. Next, by the Phoa principle

f,g:\mathbb{I}\to\Delta^{n}
are fully determined by
n
-tuples of pairs e.g.,

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\pi_{k}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\leq\pi_{k}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{k\leq n}
. In total then, we are given

n
-many 3-tuples:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\pi_{k}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\leq\pi_{k}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\pi_{k}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle g\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\leq\pi_{k}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle g\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{k\leq n}

By
Lemma

3.5
, these are 2-simplices in
\Delta^{n}
and so
every horn has a unique extension as required.
∎

We note that
Corollary

3.7
is already a significant step forward for
STT
: it
is the first result constructing an explicit example of a non-discrete category within the system.

3.3.
Reasoning with modalities in
TT
\boxbslash

A number of useful results in
TT
\boxbslash
are immediate corollaries of standard results from
MTT

combined with one of the axioms. We record some of the most important results in this section for
future use and to give a flavor for how modalities can be used to enhance simplicial reasoning.

By general results about adjoint modalities from
MTT

(
Gra+, 21
)
, we obtain
the following:

Lemma 3.8.

•

If
A\mathrel{:_{\mathsf{id}}}\mathcal{U}
,
B\mathrel{:_{\sharp}}\mathcal{U}
there is an equivalence

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp\mid\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\simeq\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp\mid B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

•

If
A\mathrel{:_{\mathsf{id}}}\mathcal{U}
,
B\mathrel{:_{\mathrm{op}}}\mathcal{U}
there is an equivalence

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\simeq\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

There are also dependent versions where e.g.,

B\mathrel{:_{\sharp}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid A$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathcal{U}
.

We record two useful consequences of the transposition principle for
\mathrm{op}\dashv\mathrm{op}
and
\flat\dashv\sharp
:

Lemma 3.9 (
Gra (23)
).

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
commutes with colimits.

Lemma 3.10.

Evaluation at endpoints
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbb{I}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\mathsf{Bool}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is an
equivalence.

A similar result is available for
\mathbb{I}\to-
in light of
Section

3.1.2
:

Lemma 3.11.

There is a unique map
{-}_{\mathbb{I}}\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\mathcal{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathcal{U}
such that the
following bijection holds:

\textstyle\prod\nolimits_{A,B\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid A^{\mathbb{I}}\to B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\simeq\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid A\to B_{\mathbb{I}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}

Consequently,
{-}_{\mathbb{I}}
preserves limits. Notably,
{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbb{I}}=\mathbf{1}
and
{-}_{\mathbb{I}}

commutes with taking fibers. These two facts imply that
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:\mathcal{U}}A$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}_{\mathbb{I}}\to\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{I}}

has small fibers and induces a dependent version of this operation
{-}_{\mathbb{I}}:\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{I}}\to\mathcal{U}
.

In more detail, there is a canonical functorial action of
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{\mathbb{I}}
which induces a map

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:\mathcal{U}_{i}}A$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}_{\mathbb{I}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{U}_{i}$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{\mathbb{I}}
. Viewed as a family of types, we therefore obtain a
map
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{U}_{i}$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{\mathbb{I}}\to\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
. We can argue that this map actually factors through

\mathcal{U}_{i}
rather than just
\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
as follows. First, by univalence the map
\mathcal{U}_{i}\to\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
is an embedding, so a factorization of
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{U}_{i}$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{\mathbb{I}}\to\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
is unique
when it exists. Using
Section

3.1.4
, it suffices to check that such a factorization
exists after restricting
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{U}_{i}$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{\mathbb{I}}\to\mathcal{U}_{i+1}
along some map

A\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathbb{I}^{n}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{U}_{i}$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{\mathbb{I}}
. Using the universal property of
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{\mathbb{I}}
, we
may factor
A
through a map
\tilde{A}_{\mathbb{I}}
where

\tilde{A}\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbb{I}^{n}$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{\mathbb{I}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{U}_{i}$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{\mathbb{I}}
. Since
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{\mathbb{I}}
preserves
fibers, it suffices to show that the pullback
P
in the following is
\mathcal{U}_{i}
-small:

P
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{f:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbb{I}^{n}$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{\mathbb{I}}}\tilde{A}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}_{\mathbb{I}}
\mathbb{I}^{n}
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbb{I}^{n}$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{\mathbb{I}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{\mathbb{I}}
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{U}_{i}$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{\mathbb{I}}
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:\mathcal{U}_{i}}A$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}_{\mathbb{I}}

Finally, we note that since the ordinary version of
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{\mathbb{I}}
preserves
\mathcal{U}_{i}
small
types,
P
is the pullback of
\mathcal{U}_{i}
-small types and is therefore itself
\mathcal{U}_{i}
-small.

To give an example of how these reasoning principles can be used, we show how they can be used to
enhance our stock of simplicial types.

Lemma 3.12.

Given
A\mathrel{:_{\mathrm{op}}}\mathcal{U}
, if
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid\mathsf{isSimp}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
then
\mathsf{isSimp}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

Proof.

Fix
i,j:\mathbb{I}
such that we must show

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is an equivalence. Using

Lemma

3.8
, the codomain is equivalent to
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid i\leq j\lor j\leq i$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
.
By
Sections

3.1.2
and
3.9
,
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid i\leq j\lor j\leq i$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
is

\neg\mathsf{mod}_{\mathrm{op}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}\geq\neg\mathsf{mod}_{\mathrm{op}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}\lor\neg\mathsf{mod}_{\mathrm{op}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}\geq\neg\mathsf{mod}_{\mathrm{op}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and the
conclusion follows immediately from our assumption
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid\mathsf{isSimp}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
.
∎

Lemma 3.13.

If
A\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}
is discrete then
A
is simplicial.

Proof.

Assume
A
is discrete, i.e.,
A\to A^{\mathbb{I}}
is an equivalence. Since cubes
separate by
Section

3.1.4
, it suffices to show for all polynomials

p,q:\mathbb{I}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\vec{x}$}\mathclose{}{]}}
in
n
variables
\vec{x}
that the map
A\to(\varphi(\vec{x})\to A)
is
an equivalence, where
\varphi(\vec{x}):=p(\vec{x})\leq q(\vec{x})\lor q(\vec{x})\leq p(\vec{x})
.

In turn, it suffices to give an
\mathbb{I}
-homotopy
h
connecting the constant map at
0
to the
identity on
\varphi(\vec{x})
, for each
\vec{x}:\mathbb{I}^{n}
. We notice that the straight-line homotopy

h(\vec{x},t)=\vec{x}\land t
from
0
to
\vec{x}
works: We have to show for each
\vec{x}
with

\varphi(\vec{x})
that
\varphi(\vec{x}\land t)
holds, for each
t
. But notice that
\varphi(0)

is true, as any pair of constants among
0,1
are comparable. By
Lemma

3.4
,

\varphi(\vec{x}\land t)
then holds for all
t
.
∎

Using the adjunction
\flat\dashv\sharp
, we can prove that e.g.,

{\mathsf{Nat}}\simeq\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid{\mathsf{Nat}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}

(
Gra, 23
)
. Accordingly, by
Section

3.1.4
:

Corollary 3.14.

{\mathsf{Nat}}
and
{\mathsf{Bool}}
are both simplicial and
\mathbb{I}
-null i.e. groupoids.

{remark}

The result analogous to
Lemma

3.13
for Rezk-complete Segal types
does not hold, falsifying a conjecture of
WL (20)
. In particular,
\Delta^{2}\mathrel{\textstyle\sqcup_{\mathbb{I}}}\Delta^{2}
can be shown to be Rezk-complete and Segal, but is not simplicial. This
same example shows that the requirement that
A
be annotated with
\flat
is necessary: as a family
over
\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}
the type
\Delta^{2}\mathrel{\textstyle\sqcup_{\mathbb{I}}}\Delta^{2}
is fiberwise a
proposition—explicitly, it is
\lambda i\,j.\,i\leq j\lor j\leq i
—and therefore it is
fiberwise
\mathbb{I}
-null. If we could apply
Lemma

3.13
without the

\flat
-annotation we could conclude that each fiber
i\leq j\lor j\leq i
was simplicial. Combined
with the fact that
\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}
is simplicial, this leads again to the false conclusion that

\Delta^{2}\mathrel{\textstyle\sqcup_{\mathbb{I}}}\Delta^{2}
is simplicial.

4.
The cubical spaces model

TT
\boxbslash
is intended to be an internal language for cubical sets i.e.
\mathrm{PSh}(\square)
(or rather its

\infty
-categorical enhancement). In order to make this precise, we construct a model of
TT
\boxbslash
in
which types are realized as (families of)
\infty
-presheaves over the Dedekind cube category.
Immediately, we must contend with the fact that syntax is 1-categorical and models of
syntax
(
Gra+, 21
)
are also inherently 1-categorical. To overcome this
mismatch, we interpret
TT
\boxbslash
into a model category which presents the appropriate presheaf

\infty
-category
(
Shu, 15
;
KL, 21
;
Shu, 19
)
.

For
TT
\boxbslash
, this model category will be the
injective model structure on simplicial presheaves
on Dedekind cubes

\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{sSet}}(\square)
. That is, types in
TT
\boxbslash
are interpreted as certain
families of presheaves over
\square
valued in
\mathrm{sSet}=\mathrm{PSh}(\mathbbl{\Delta})
. It is helpful to view the
simplicial sets layer as “mixing in” homotopy theory with ordinary presheaves over
\square
.

The construction of the intended model of
TT
\boxbslash
is largely an exercise in combining off-the-shelf
results about models of
HoTT
and models of
MTT
. In particular,
Shu (19)
shows
that
HoTT
admits a model in
\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{sSet}}(\square)
and results of
Shu (23)
and

Gra (23)
show that this model refines to a model of
MTT
. One then directly verifies
that this model validates the additional axioms required by
TT
\boxbslash
. However, for the sake of
completeness we give some of the details of this process. In particular, in
Section

4.1
we
spell out the definition of a model of
MTT
. In
Section

4.2
we show how to apply a
theorem of
Shu (23)
to construct a model of
MTT
and verify that it extends
appropriately to the desired model of
TT
\boxbslash
.

{remark}

Models of
TT
\boxbslash
specifically and type theory generally contain quite a lot of data to account for
each connective. We will focus primarily on the specific modal connectives in
TT
\boxbslash
in this
section, as—just as in the syntax of
TT
\boxbslash
itself—the other connectives are treated in a
totally standard manner.

4.1.
Models of
MTT
and
TT
\boxbslash

In this subsection, we briefly recall the main aspects of the model theory of
MTT
. We shall not
directly use these definitions in our construction of the cubical spaces model of
TT
\boxbslash
and instead
will rely on a general
coherence
result of Shulman. Accordingly, these
definitions are recalled only to make the following discussion more concrete.

When originally introduced by
Gra+ (21)
,
MTT
was presented as a certain

generalized algebraic theory
(GAT). Consequently, the general theory of GATs ensures that
there is a category of models of
MTT
(parameterized by the chosen mode theory) which refines the
corresponding category of models of ordinary Martin-Löf type theory
(
Dyb, 96
)
.
Let us therefore begin by recalling the definition of a model of dependent type theory:

Definition 4.1 (
Awo (18)
).

A category with families (CwF)
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{C},\tau$}\mathclose{}{)}}
consists of a category
\mathcal{C}
along with a morphism

in
\mathrm{PSh}(\mathcal{C})
is equipped with the following chosen
data:
9

9

The fact that this is chosen data rather than a mere existence property is a quirk
of generalized algebraic theories. More refined recent approaches such as those given by

Uem (21)
do not have this deficiency.

•

\mathcal{C}
has a chosen terminal object,

•

\tau
is locally representable i.e., the fiber
\mathbf{y}(c)\times_{U}U^{\bullet}
has a
chosen representation
\mathbf{y}(c^{\prime})
for each morphism
\mathbf{y}(c)
U
.

This structure is a model of dependent type theory with no connectives, only basic operations like
context extension, variables, and substitutions. We refer the reader to
Awo (18)
for a
careful exposition of how connectives may be integrated into this definition. Since this aspect of
CwFs is carried through unchanged through the remainder of our discussion, we ignore it here.

A model of
MTT
elaborates on this structure by linking together CwFs via functors (which
intuitively model
\Gamma/\mu
). We begin with the structure necessary to model
MTT
without any
connectives.

Note that

additional requirement that each

annotated
variables in a context i.e.
\Gamma,x\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A
.
Other connectives are integrated into this definition without change. For instance, the inclusion of

\textstyle\sum\nolimits
-types is accounted for by requiring that each
\tau_{m}
is closed under
\textstyle\sum\nolimits
-types. This
process is entirely mechanical and their interpretation in the intended model of
TT
\boxbslash
is
unsurprising, accordingly we refer the reader to
Gra+ (21)
for further
information.

The central novel connective of
MTT
—the modal types—is slightly more complex, and so we
briefly touch on their definition. We refer the reader to
Gra+ (21)
or

Gra (23)
for a careful discussion of how the following definition relates to the
syntax introduced in
Section

3
.

, we require a
stable weak orthogonality
structure

(
Awo, 18
)

s:m\mathrel{\pitchfork}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{*}U_{n}$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{*}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\tau_{m}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
in

\mathrm{PSh}(F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}})/F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{*}U_{n}
.

Roughly, the first point encodes the introduction and formation rules of the
\mu
modal type and
the second encodes the elimination rule and its attendant equality.

We will not spend much time with this definition because, by a result of
Shu (23)
,
one can construct a model of
MTT
with all connectives from far more recognizable data. In
particular, op. cit. adapts the local universes coherence construction of
LW (15)

which promotes type-theoretic fibration categories to models.

Definition 4.4.

A type-theoretic fibration category consists of a category
\mathcal{C}
with all finite limits
with a chosen class of morphisms
\mathcal{F}
referred to as fibrations which satisfy the
following closure conditions:

•

\mathcal{F}
is closed under identity and composition.

•

\mathcal{F}
is closed under pullback along arbitrary maps.

•

\mathcal{F}
is closed under pushforwards; if
f\in\mathcal{F}
then the right adjoint
to pullback along
f
sends fibrations to fibrations.

•

Every morphism
f
admits a factorization
f^{\prime}\circ i
such that
f^{\prime}
is a fibration and

i
is weakly left orthogonal to fibrations and this factorization is stable under pullbacks.

We refer to maps weakly left orthogonal to fibrations as
anodyne
.

4.2.
Constructing the cubical spaces model

First, we require the following result fundamental result from
Shu (19)
:

Definition 4.7.

Let
\square
be the category of Dedekind cubes i.e., the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Cat}
generated by
finite products of the category
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{]}}=\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0\to 1$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
.

Proposition 4.8 (
Shu (19)
).

\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{sSet}}(\square)
and the collection of injective fibrations forms a type-theoretic fibration
category with a hierarchy of univalent universes.

We will now use this type-theoretic fibration category as the basic input for

Proposition

4.6
. In particular, we consider the functor
F:\hbox to47.95pt{\vbox to9.73pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.45834pt\lower-2.9pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{}{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

sends the unique object

to

F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

Proof.

First, we note that the equations for
\mathcal{M}_{\boxbslash}
ensure that we need only consider

\mu,\nu\in\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op},\sharp,\flat,\mathsf{id}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
. Further, by adjointness, we may ignore the cases where

\nu\in\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp,\mathrm{op}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
. We check the remaining cases directly.

For instance, if
\mu=\nu=\mathsf{id}
, then we note that a natural transformation

F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is determined by its behavior at
\mathbf{y}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{]}})
; both sides
preserve products, tensoring by simplicial sets, and colimits and all cubical spaces are generated
from
\mathbf{y}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{]}})
under these operations. However, there is only one morphism

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{]}}
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{]}}
which preserves endpoints: the identity map. The same reasoning rules out
the possibility of any natural transformations with
\mu=\mathsf{id},\nu=\mathrm{op}
or
\mu=\mathsf{id},\nu=\flat
. It also guarantees that there is exactly one natural transformation

F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{id}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

The case where
\mu=\flat
is similar, so we focus on the remaining case where
\mu=\sharp
. This
case is slightly more complex, since
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp$}\mathclose{}{)}}
does not preserve colimits and so we can
immediately reduce to checking behavior at
\mathbf{y}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{]}})
. However, we can immediately rule out
natural transformations
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp$}\mathclose{}{)}}
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{id}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
,
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp$}\mathclose{}{)}}
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, or

F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp$}\mathclose{}{)}}
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat$}\mathclose{}{)}}
by considering the behavior of such morphisms at
\mathbf{y}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{]}})
.
To show that there is exactly one morphism
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp$}\mathclose{}{)}}
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, we note that it
suffices to show that there is exactly one natural transformation

Proof.

We begin by noting that while
p^{\prime}=F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat$}\mathclose{}{)}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is not necessarily an injective fibration,
it is a
projective
fibration. Indeed,
p
is a projective fibration so that, by definition,

p\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is a fibration and so
p^{\prime}
is a levelwise (i.e., projective) fibration. We may
now apply the cobar construction detailed by
Shu (19, Definition 8.17)
to obtain a
stable factorization of
p^{\prime}
into a trivial cofibration
i
followed by an injective fibration

f
, as required.

For the second condition, we note that
p^{\prime}
and
f
are both projective fibrations and,
consequently, so too are
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{)}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{)}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. Moreover, since
injective trivial cofibrations are precisely a levelwise trivial cofibration, we may also conclude
that
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{)}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is a injective trivial cofibration—note that trivial cofibrations are
closed under cartesian products in
\mathrm{sSet}
for the case where
\nu=\sharp
. Finally, since
pullbacks are computed levelwise and the model structure on
\mathrm{sSet}
is right proper, we see that

z^{*}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{)}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
remains a trivial cofibration for all

Lemma 4.12.

There is a model of
MTT
with mode theory
\mathcal{M}_{\boxbslash}
in
\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{sSet}}(\square)
which
interprets modalities using the functors described by
F

Finally, we must show that this model of
MTT
validates the axioms necessary for
TT
\boxbslash
.

Section

3.1.4
is an immediate consequence of
Shu (19)
. Notably,

Gra (23)
shows that
Section

3.1.4
holds for
\sharp
,
\mathrm{op}
, and

\mathsf{id}
—since they are
dependent right adjoints
—and that it holds for
\flat
since it
is an internal left adjoint.

Sections

3.1.2
,
3.1.2
,
3.1.4
and
3.1.4
are all statements about
sets—
0
-truncated types—and so hold in the above model if and only if they hold in the ordinary
interpretation of type theory in
\mathrm{PSh}(\square)
. That is, for these axioms we may ignore
the simplicial dimension of
\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{sSet}}(\square)
. In this case, each of these except

Section

3.1.4
is a routine verification.

10

10

See also
(
Mye, 25
)
for a general topos-theoretic account.
The duality axiom, finally, follows from a result of

Ble (23, Theorem 4.11)
combined with the following lemma:

Lemma 4.13.

There is a canonical geometric embedding
\mathrm{PSh}(\square)
\mathrm{Set}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{DLat}$}\mathclose{}{]}}
of cubical
sets into the classifying topos of distributive lattices. Under this embedding,
\mathbf{y}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{]}})

in
\mathrm{PSh}(\square)
is sent to the generic bounded distributive lattice.

The remaining axioms
(
Sections

3.1.2
,
3.1.4
,
3.1.4
and
3.1.4
) are
verified by straightforward (if tedious) categorical arguments. In each case, we must show
that a certain function in type theory is an equivalence. After unfolding to the model, this amounts
to showing that a certain morphism is a weak equivalence. In each case, these statements admit
natural
\infty
-categorical interpretations (e.g.,
Section

3.1.2
postulates the
existence of an amazing right adjoint to
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{\mathbb{I}}
), but a model-categorical argument is
necessary to connect this fact to the interpretation of
TT
\boxbslash
in
\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{sSet}}(\square)
. Since these
arguments are nearly all the same, we choose to focus on the most complex:

Section

3.1.2
.

Lemma 4.14.

The following type is inhabited in the model of
MTT
in
\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{sSet}}(\square)
:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A_{\mathbb{I}}\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}}\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{\epsilon\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A_{\mathbb{I}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{\mathbb{I}}\to A}\textstyle\prod\nolimits_{B\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}}\mathsf{isEquiv}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid B\to A_{\mathbb{I}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid B^{\mathbb{I}}\to A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

Proof.

For concision, let us denote the codomain of the above function by
\mathsf{isRepr}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.
First, we note that we may prove directly in
MTT
that
\mathsf{isRepr}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is a proposition,
so this type is inhabited if and only if the following fibration is a trivial fibration in our
model:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big\llbracket}{\big\llbracket}{\big\llbracket}{\llbracket}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{\mathsf{mod}_{\flat}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\mathcal{U}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}}\mathsf{isRepr}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big\rrbracket}{\big\rrbracket}{\big\rrbracket}{\rrbracket}}}
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\mathcal{U}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}

To prove this, we may restrict our attention to showing that the restriction of this fibration
along the trivial cofibration

\mathsf{Const}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{U}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\mathcal{U}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}
is trivial. Here
\mathsf{Const}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle X$}\mathclose{}{)}}
denotes the constant cubical space with value
X
.

It suffices to check that this morphism is a trivial fibration fiberwise. We therefore need to
show that the restriction of this map along

A:\hbox to114.93pt{\vbox to10pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 12.70256pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{}{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-12.70256pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -17.58 0)} \pgfsys@hbox{59}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{29.61919pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 40.98 0)} \pgfsys@hbox{59}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
