is a trivial fibration. However,

A
factors through
\mathbf{y}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{]}})
. Consequently, we may restrict our attention to the case where

n=0
.

Accordingly, we may fix
X\in\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{U}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
i.e. an injectively fibrant presheaf

X
and we must show that
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{isRepr}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}_{A\mapsto X}
is inhabited. We now unfold

\mathsf{isRepr}
to see that it suffices to construct another injectively fibrant presheaf
Y
such
that for all fibrant
Z
, there is a natural weak equivalence

\hom\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle Z^{\mathbf{y}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{]}})},X$}\mathclose{}{)}}
\hom\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle Z,X^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

Let us now note that
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{\mathbf{y}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{]}})}\cong\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{*}
. This is a left adjoint
which preserves injective cofibrations and therefore has a right adjoint
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{\mathbf{y}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{]}})}

which preserves injective fibrations. We choose
X^{\prime}=\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle X$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{\mathbf{y}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{]}})}
and the rest
follows.
∎

Theorem 4.15.

TT
\boxbslash
has a model in
\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{sSet}}(\square)
where types are injective fibrations and modalities are
interpreted as described above.

Crucially, within this model simplicial types are precisely those belonging to the subtopos

\mathrm{PSh}_{\mathrm{sSet}}(\mathbbl{\Delta})

(
SW, 21
)
. Consequently, the adequacy result from

RS (17)
applies and we conclude that this model shows that any fact proven about
categories and groupoids inside of
TT
\boxbslash
is a valid proof for the standard definition of

\infty
-categories.

Theorem 4.16.

Categories in
TT
\boxbslash
adequately model
\infty
-categories.

5.
Covariant and amazingly covariant families

In
Section

2
, we saw how groupoids were defined internally as those types satisfying

\mathsf{isGroupoid}\,A=\mathsf{isEquiv}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A\to A^{\mathbb{I}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. We might hope this induces a
directed univalent universe of groupoids directly, by considering

\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{grp}}=\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:\mathcal{U}}\mathsf{isGroupoid}\,A
. However, this is far from our desired
universe. Most glaringly, while
F:A\to\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{grp}}
is a family of groupoids over
A
, this
family is not required to respect the category structure of
A
in any way. In fact, one may show
that a map
F:\mathbb{I}\to\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{grp}}
is akin to an unstructured relation between
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}

and
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and nothing like the function required for directed univalence.

Example 5.1.

By assumption,
\mathbb{I}
is a set and so
f=\lambda i.\,i=0
is a function
\mathbb{I}\to\mathsf{HProp}
. Since
each proposition is a groupoid, this ensures that
f
factors through
\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{grp}}
despite
the fact that there can be no function from
f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

In order to rectify this and define
{\mathcal{S}}
, we shall require a theory of families of groupoids
where a morphism
f:\hom\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a,a^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
in
A
induces a functor of groupoids
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

RS (17)
termed these
covariant
families and they are further studied by

BW (23)
. As mentioned in the introduction, we shall also require a modal version
of covariant families
F:A\to\mathcal{U}
which are covariant not only in
A
but also in the entire
context.

5.1.
Covariant families and transport

We begin by recalling the definition of a covariant family from
RS (17)
.

Definition 5.2.

A family
A:X\to\mathcal{U}
is
covariant
if the following proposition holds:

\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\textstyle\prod\nolimits_{x:\mathbb{I}\to X}\textstyle\prod\nolimits_{a_{0}:A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x\,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\mathsf{isContr}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{a_{1}:A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x\,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\hom_{A}^{x}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a_{0},a_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}

{convention}

While not strictly necessary, we will assume that the base of a covariant family
A
is a Segal
type unless explicitly noted otherwise.

We recall a few facts about covariant families (also due to
RS (17)
).

Lemma 5.3.

If
A:X\to\mathcal{U}
is covariant and
f:Y\to X
then
A\circ f
is also covariant.

Lemma 5.4.

Given
\phi:\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A:X\to\mathcal{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and
f:\hom\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x_{0},x_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
then there is an induced
transport map
\mathsf{coe}_{A\circ f}:A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. Moreover, transport maps respect
composition and identities.

Proof Sketch.

One defines the transport map
\mathsf{coe}_{A\circ f}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\pi_{1}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\phi\,f\,a_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}
.
We leave it to the reader to check that this has the appropriate type and that the expected
identities are satisfied.
∎

Lemma 5.5.

Given
\phi:\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A:X\to\mathcal{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and

x:X
, the fiber
A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is a groupoid.

Proof.

Since covariant families stable under base-change,
A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is a covariant family
\mathbf{1}\to\mathcal{U}
. Unfolding definitions, we conclude that the following holds:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a:A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{isContr}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{f:\mathbb{I}\to A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}=a$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}

This is equivalent to the proposition that

\mathsf{eval}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbb{I}\to A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is an equivalence. By 3-for-2, this
implies that
\mathsf{const}:A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{\mathbb{I}}
is an equivalence, as

\mathsf{eval}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}\circ\mathsf{const}=\mathsf{id}
.
∎

It is often helpful to rephrase covariant families in terms of orthogonality conditions:

Definition 5.6.

Given a type family
A:X\to\mathcal{U}
, we shall write
\tilde{A}
for the
total type

\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{x:X}A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

Lemma 5.7 (
RS, 17
, Theorem 8.5
).

A family
A:X\to\mathcal{U}
is covariant if and only if the projection map
\tilde{A}\to X
is
right orthogonal to
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
\mathbb{I}
i.e., if
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\tilde{A}$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{\mathbb{I}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\tilde{A}$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}}\times_{X^{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}}}X^{\mathbb{I}}
is an equivalence.

This formulation also makes plainer the fact that covariant families are the simplicial type theory
analogue of left fibrations in ordinary
\infty
-category theory
(
Joy, 08
;
Lur, 09
)
.

Finally, using the characterization of covariance as an orthogonality condition, we are able to
prove the following:

Lemma 5.8.

If
A\mathrel{:_{\flat}}X\to\mathcal{U}
is covariant then
A
is simplicial i.e., it factors through

\mathcal{U}_{\boxbslash}\to\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

By
Section

3.1.4
, it suffices to show the following:

\mathsf{isEquiv}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\langle}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\mathbb{I}^{n}\to\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{x:X}\mathsf{isSimp}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\rangle}}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\mathbb{I}^{n}\to X$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}

In particular, we may assume that
X=\mathbb{I}^{n}
by restricting
A
and so we hereafter also
assume that
X
is simplicial.

With this in mind,
A
factors through
\mathcal{U}_{\boxbslash}
if and only if
\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{x:X}A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is
simplicial. Next, we observe that
\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{x:X}A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is simplicial if and only if the
projection map
\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{x:X}A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to X
is right orthogonal to

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}i\leq j\lor j\leq i$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}\to\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}
.

Since
A
and
X
are both
\flat
-annotated, we may use
Section

3.1.4
again to reduce to
showing that
\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{x:X}A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to X
is
\flat
-orthogonal to

\mathbb{I}^{n}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}i\leq j\lor j\leq i$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}\to\mathbb{I}^{n+2}
.
We will now argue that this map is in the left class generated by the inclusion
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\to\mathbb{I}^{n}
; we know that
\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{x:X}A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to X
is right orthogonal to such maps by virtue of
our assumption that
A
was covariant.

To this end, we consider the canonical inclusions

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\to\mathbb{I}^{n}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}i\leq j\lor j\leq i$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}

and
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\to\mathbb{I}^{n+2}
.
Using the 3-for-2 property available for the left class of maps, to show that

\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{x:X}A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to X
is right orthogonal to

\mathbb{I}^{n}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}i\leq j\lor j\leq i$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}\to\mathbb{I}^{n+2}
,
it suffices to show that (1) it is orthogonal to
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\to\mathbb{I}^{n+2}
and (2)
it is orthogonal to

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\to\mathbb{I}^{n}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}i\leq j\lor j\leq i$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}
.
The first claim is immediate from our assumption that
A
is covariant.

For the second claim, we note that

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\to\mathbb{I}^{n}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}i\leq j\lor j\leq i$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}
is the pushout of the maps

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\to\mathbb{I}^{n}\times\mathbb{I}
and
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\to\mathbb{I}^{n}\times\Delta^{2}
. We note that the latter
is a retract of
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\to\mathbb{I}^{n}\times\mathbb{I}^{2}
and so both of these maps are orthogonal to

\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{x:X}A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to X
, again by our assumption that
A
is covariant. The conclusion follows
by the closure of left classes of maps under colimits.
∎

5.2.
Amazing covariance

We now refine our search from a universe of groupoids to a universe of
covariant fibrations
.
That is, we wish to define some universe
{\mathcal{S}}
such that a map
A\to{\mathcal{S}}
corresponds (in
some sense) to a covariant fibration over
A
. Let us leave this correspondence imprecise for now
and consider the behavior of
{\mathcal{S}}
.

In light of
Lemma

5.5
, the points of
{\mathcal{S}}
will be
covariant over
\mathbf{1}
i.e. groupoids. However, elements
f:\mathbb{I}\to{\mathcal{S}}
will
become richer: they are covariant fibrations
B\to\mathbb{I}
, therefore consist not only of a pair of
groupoids
B_{0},B_{1}
over
0
and
1
, but also include a transport function
B_{0}\to B_{1}

(
Lemma

5.4
). Phrased differently, a homomorphism
F:\mathbb{I}\to{\mathcal{S}}

contains an ordinary function
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

Clearly this is a step towards directed univalence over
\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:\mathcal{U}}\mathsf{isGroupoid}\,A
, but it
is far from obvious how to define such a type
{\mathcal{S}}
. In particular, while we have sketched how
behavior ought to differ between elements of
{\mathcal{S}}
compared with functions
\mathbb{I}\to{\mathcal{S}}
and
so on, we cannot really cleanly divide elements of
{\mathcal{S}}
from functions into
{\mathcal{S}}
within type
theory. Within dependent type theory, every element of
{\mathcal{S}}
is formed to some context
\Gamma

and if that
\Gamma
happens contains a variable
i:\mathbb{I}
, then this term will induce a function

\mathbb{I}\to{\mathcal{S}}
.

There is an even more straightforward way to see why this causes a problem. Suppose we attempt to
define another subtype of
\mathcal{U}
to isolate this universe of covariant fibrations

\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:\mathcal{U}}\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. A cursory inspection reveals this to be nonsense: being
covariant is not a property of
A
, it is a property of a family of types
A:X\to\mathcal{U}
. So in
this ‘definition’, what exactly is
A
covariant over?

It is here that modalities are vital:
A
should be covariant with respect to the entire ambient
context. This is not something that can be expressed in standard type theory, but with the amazing
right adjoint to
\mathbb{I}\to-
we are able to define such a subtype.

Types covariant over
\Gamma

We define a predicate on types
\mathsf{isACov}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{HProp}

which encodes whether a type is covariant over the entire context following
Ril (24)
.
We note that this predicate is a refinement of
Lic+ (18)
which capitalizes on the
existence of the amazing right adjoint as a proper modality. The construction of this predicate
proceeds in two steps:

(1)

We begin by observing that
\mathsf{isCov}_{X}
has the type
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle X\to\mathcal{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{HProp}
for each type
X:\mathcal{U}
. In particular,

\mathsf{isCov}_{\mathbb{I}}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbb{I}\to\mathcal{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{HProp}

(2)

As
\mathsf{isCov}_{\mathbb{I}}
, we may apply
Lemma

3.11
to obtain a function

\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{HProp}_{\mathbb{I}}
.

(3)

Finally, we post-compose with the dependent version of
(-)_{\mathbb{I}}
to construct a function

\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{HProp}
.

All told, we obtain a predicate
\mathsf{isACov}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathsf{HProp}
which encodes whether a given type is covariant
over the entire context.

Definition 5.9.

A type is said to be
amazingly covariant
when it satisfies
\mathsf{isACov}
.

We begin by substantiating the claim that
\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
implies that
A
is truly covariant over
all variables in the context.

Lemma 5.10.

Given
F:X\to\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:\mathcal{U}}\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, the type family
F_{0}=\pi_{1}\circ F
is
covariant.

{notation}

We will write
\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}
for the subtype
\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:\mathcal{U}}\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

Proof.

We must show
\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle F_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. First, we note that since
X\to\mathcal{U}
being covariant
implies that the composite
Y\to X\to\mathcal{U}
is covariant for all
Y\to X
, we may reduce to the generic case
where
X=\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}
and, in particular, where
F
is
\flat
-annotated.

Next, note that
\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}
fits into the following pullback diagram:

\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}
\mathcal{U}
{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbb{I}}
\mathsf{HProp}_{\mathbb{I}}
{\mathsf{isCov}}^{\leftarrow}

We therefore note that
X\to\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}
is equivalent to asking for a pair of maps
F_{0}:X\to\mathcal{U}
and
F_{1}:X\to{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbb{I}}
such that the induced maps
X\to\mathsf{HProp}_{\mathbb{I}}
agree.
Since
X\to\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}
along with the above pullback diagram consists only of
\flat
-annotated
objects, we may therefore transpose to conclude that the induced maps
X^{\mathbb{I}}\to\mathsf{HProp}
agree.
Unfolding, these maps are given as follows:

\displaystyle\lambda f:X^{\mathbb{I}}.\,\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle F_{0}\circ f$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle\lambda f:X^{\mathbb{I}}.\,\mathbf{1}

Consequently, that these two maps agree amounts to a proof that
F_{0}
is covariant, as required.
∎

We emphasize that in the above
\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}
does not “know about”
X
. In particular, this is a
subtype of
\mathcal{U}
such that any map into this subtype induces covariant families.

Finally, the additional burden of being covariant over the context does not apply when working under

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
, a reflection of the fact that
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
is “a proof of
A
not depending
on the context.”

Lemma 5.11.

If
X\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}
and

A\mathrel{:_{\flat}}X\to\mathcal{U}
then
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:X$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}=\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
.

Proof.

Since both
\mathsf{isACov}
and
\mathsf{isCov}
are propositions, it suffices to construct a bi-implication.
First, let us suppose that
z\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:X$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
holds.
Applying the introduction rule for
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
, we wish to show that
\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}

holds. Let us note that
z
implies that
A:X\to\mathcal{U}
factors through
\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}
. That is,
we have a diagram of the following shape (consisting of
\flat
-types and functions):

\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}
\mathcal{U}
{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbb{I}}
\mathsf{HProp}_{\mathbb{I}}
{\mathsf{isCov}}^{\leftarrow}
X
A

By the naturality of transposition, we conclude that

\mathsf{isCov}\circ A_{*}=\lambda\_.\,\mathbf{1}
as functions from
X^{\mathbb{I}}\to\mathsf{HProp}
.
Consequently,
A
is covariant as required.

For the reverse direction, suppose that
z\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. It suffices to
construct a (necessarily unique) function
A_{0}\mathrel{:_{\flat}}X\to\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}
with an
identification
\pi\circ A_{0}=A
. By the universal property of pullbacks along with the
identification
\mathbf{1}={\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbb{I}}
, it therefore suffices to construct an
identification between
\mathsf{isCov}_{\mathbb{I}}\circ A
and
\lambda\_.\,{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbb{I}}
. After
transposing, we therefore must show that the map
X^{\mathbb{I}}\to\mathsf{HProp}
sending
x:\mathbb{I}\to X
to

\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A\circ x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is equal to the map sending
x:\mathbb{I}\to X
to
\mathbf{1}
. This,
finally, follows immediately from our assumption that
\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
holds.
∎

5.3.
Closure properties of amazing covariance

Given the strength of
\mathsf{isACov}
, the reader may wonder how one ever proves that
\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}

for any element
A:\mathcal{U}
. In this section, we give a partial answer by building up a stock of
amazingly covariant types. We shall see in
Section

6
that these results undergird the closure
properties of our directed univalent universe. Our main result is the following:

Lemma 5.12.

In what follows, let us assume that
A,A_{0},A_{1}:\mathcal{U}
and
B:A\to\mathcal{U}
.

(1)

If
X\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}
then
\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid X$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

(2)

If
i:\mathbb{I}
then
\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i=1$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

(3)

If
\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and
a,b:A
then
\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a=b$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

(4)

If
\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
then

\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{a:A}B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}
.

(5)

If
\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
,
\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and
f,g:A_{0}\to A_{1}
then

\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{Coeq}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.
11
11

11

Here
\mathsf{Coeq}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}
denotes the coequalizer of
f,g

realized as a higher-inductive type
(
Uni, 13
)
.

Moreover,
\mathsf{isACov}
is closed under
\Pi
-types provided modalities are used to
manage the variance swap:

(6)

If
C\mathrel{:_{\mathrm{op}}}\mathcal{U}
and
D:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid A$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathcal{U}
such that
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}

and
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle c\mathrel{:_{\mathrm{op}}}C$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle D\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle c$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
then

\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle c\mathrel{:_{\mathrm{op}}}C$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to D\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle c$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

We record a useful special case of (6) which follows from the involutive property of
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
:

Corollary 5.13.

If
X\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}
,
B:X\to\mathcal{U}
such that
\textstyle\prod\nolimits_{x:X}\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

then
\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\prod\nolimits_{x:X}B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}
.

We limit ourselves to proving three representative cases of the above theorem: (2), (4), (6). These
are indicative of the remaining cases (and those we have particular use for in

Section

6
).

Lemma 5.14.

If
i:\mathbb{I}
then
\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i=1$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

Proof.

To prove this result, we shall switch to a more general goal,

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i=1$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
, which can then be specialized to yield the
original result. Using
Lemma

3.11
, it suffices to construct an element of

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f:\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\lambda j.\,f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}=1$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}

Since we have no additional hypotheses in this proof, we may forget the
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
and assume

f:\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{I}
. By
Section

3.1.2
,
\mathbb{I}
is an h-set and so
\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}=1$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is
equivalent to showing that
f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}=1
implies that
f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}=1
i.e. that
f
is
monotone. This is an immediate consequence of
Lemma

3.4
.
∎

Lemma 5.15.

If
\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
then
\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{a:A}B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}
.

Proof.

As before, we begin by generalizing slightly and instead proving the following:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\langle}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,B$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}}\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}^{A}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}\to\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{a:A}B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\rangle}}}

Let us begin by applying (the dependent version of)
Lemma

3.11
such that it
suffices to show that the following holds instead:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\langle}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,B$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}^{\mathbb{I}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}\to\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\lambda i.\,\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{a:A\,i}B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i,a$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\rangle}}}

Since we have no additional assumptions, we may drop the
\flat
and assume we are given
A:\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}^{\mathbb{I}}
and
B:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to A\,i\to\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}
. In light of

Lemma

5.10
, we note that
A
is covariant and, moreover, so
too is
\lambda i.\,B\,i\,\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a\,i$}\mathclose{}{)}}
for any
a:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to A\,i
.

In total then, we are reduced to proving the following: if
A:\mathbb{I}\to\mathcal{U}
and
B:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to A\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and
\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\lambda i.\,B\,i\,\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a\,i$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
then
\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\lambda i.\,\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{a:A\,i}B\,i\,a$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}
. This statement is
proven by
BW (23, Proposition 6.2.1)
.
∎

For clarity, we show the proof of the non-dependent version of (6). This is all that is required in
the next section and illustrates the core idea with less noise from handling indices.

Lemma 5.16.

If
C\mathrel{:_{\mathrm{op}}}\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}
and
D:\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}
then
\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid C$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to D$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

Proof.

Following the previous two arguments, we will begin by proving this in a
\flat
-context to deal
with
\mathsf{isACov}
. That is, we first prove the following:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle C\mathrel{:_{\mathrm{op}}}\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle D:\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid C$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to D$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}

Arguing as in
Lemma

5.15
, we may use

Lemma

5.10
and
Lemma

3.11
to assume that
we are given
C\mathrel{:_{\mathrm{op}}}\mathbb{I}\to\mathcal{U}
and
D:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathcal{U}

such that (1)
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
and (2)
\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle D$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and such that we
must show (3) that
\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\lambda i.\,\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\neg i$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to D\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

We begin by noting that
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
is equivalent to the following assumption:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle c_{1}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{isContr}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{c:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\neg i$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}}c\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}=c_{1}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}

Unfolding our obligation, we must show that given
f_{0}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to{D\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}}
that
the following type is contractible:

\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{f:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\neg i$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to D\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}}f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}=f_{0}

We first informally describe how one produces a center of contraction. Given
i:\mathbb{I}
and
c:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\neg i$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
, we use the assumption that
C
is
\mathrm{op}
-covariant as formulated
above to construct a (unique) function
\bar{c}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle j:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\neg\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i\land j$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
such that
\bar{c}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}=c
. We then observe that
\bar{c}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
and so
d_{0}=f_{0}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\bar{c}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}:D\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0,\bar{c}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.
Extending this
d_{0}
to a line along
D\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i\land-$}\mathclose{}{)}}
gives the line
\bar{d}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle j:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to D\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle j\land i$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and we choose
f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i,c$}\mathclose{}{)}}=f_{0}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\bar{d}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle c:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\neg i$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to D\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i,c$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. If
i=0
,
\bar{c}
and
\bar{d}
are canonically
equal to constant functions and these identifications combine to produce a path
p:f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}=f_{0}
.

If we write
\widebar{\mathsf{coe}}_{C}
for the “backwards” coercion function induced by

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid\mathsf{isCov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle C$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
, we may describe
f
symbolically as follows:

f=\lambda i\,c.\mathsf{coe}_{D\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i\land-$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f_{0}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\widebar{\mathsf{coe}}_{C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\neg i\land-$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle c$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

Suppose now we are given
g:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\neg i$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to D\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}
along
with
p:g\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}=f_{0}
. We must then show that
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f,p$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle g,q$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. Let us consider the
following
H
:

\displaystyle H:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i\,j:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\neg i$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to D\,i

\displaystyle H=\lambda i\,j,c.\mathsf{coe}_{D\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle(i\land j)\lor-\land i$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle g\,(i\land j)\,\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\widebar{\mathsf{coe}}_{C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\neg(i\land j)\land-\lor\neg i$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle c$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

We may construct a function
r:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle j:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to H\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0,j$}\mathclose{}{)}}=f_{0}
using
p
and
q
. Moreover,
we may identify
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle H\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-,0$}\mathclose{}{)}},r\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
with
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f,p$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle H\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-,1$}\mathclose{}{)}},r\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

with
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle g,q$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. Finally, since the type
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\neg i$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to D\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is a groupoid
(
RSS, 20
, Lemma 1.26)
and groupoids are closed under

\Sigma
-types,
\lambda j.\,\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle H\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-,j$}\mathclose{}{)}},r\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
induces the required identification
between
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f,p$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle g,q$}\mathclose{}{)}}
as required.
∎

These proofs exhibit proof strategies that are common when working with
\mathsf{isACov}
in
TT
\boxbslash
:
either reducing to a generic global case where various modalities can be simplified or performing
several small modal manipulations and then applying standard and non-modal arguments. They are
also very similar to the construction of fibrancy structures in
WL (20)
. The major
difference between the proofs given in op. cit. stems from the fact that our constructions take
place in a univalent type theory. Consequently, our coercion operators have a simpler type, but we
must show that they are unique up to a contractible choice rather than merely having to construct
some inhabitant.

6.
The directed univalent universe

With our preliminary work on amazing covariance in place, we are now in a position to define our
directed univalent universe of groupoids
{\mathcal{S}}
and establish its core properties. We begin with
the (now short) definition of
{\mathcal{S}}
:

Definition 6.1.

We define
{\mathcal{S}}
to be
\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:\mathcal{U}}{\mathsf{isACov}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}
.

We note that
{\mathcal{S}}
can be fully characterized without reference to

\mathsf{ACov}
as a corollary of
Lemma

5.11
:
12
12

12

Theoretically, every result about
{\mathcal{S}}
can be proven using this
characterization. We will not endeavor to do so and instead optimize for more readable proofs.

Lemma 6.2.

If
A\mathrel{:_{\flat}}X\to\mathcal{U}
then
A
factors
through
{\mathcal{S}}
if and only if it is covariant. In other words,
{\mathcal{S}}
is the base of the
universal covariant family of simplicial types.

Corollary 6.3.

A\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}
factors through
{\mathcal{S}}
if and only if it is a groupoid.

Applying
Lemma

5.8
, we conclude that
{\mathcal{S}}\to\mathcal{U}
factors through
\mathcal{U}_{\boxbslash}
. In other words, all amazingly covariant families of groupoids
are automatically simplicial. Moreover, by
Lemma

5.12
along with the closure
results from
RSS (20)
, we conclude:

Lemma 6.4.

As a subtype of
\mathcal{U}
,
{\mathcal{S}}
is (1) univalent (2) contains only simplicial types (3) closed
under dependent sums, equality, and
i=1
(4) closed under the two modalized forms of

\Pi
-types indicated by
Lemma

5.12
.

Thus, we already have established that
{\mathcal{S}}
is a subuniverse of
\mathcal{U}
spanned by
groupoids. What remains is to prove directed univalence i.e., to characterize
\mathbb{I}\to{\mathcal{S}}
. To
this end, we will first prove two important lemmas for constructing elements of
{\mathcal{S}}
. With these
in place, we shall show that
{\mathcal{S}}
is not only closed under various connectives, but also
simplicial, Segal, Rezk, and directed univalent. Our main result can be summed up as follows

Theorem 6.5.

{\mathcal{S}}
is a directed-univalent category.

{remark}

While it is not helpful for establishing the above theorem, the following observation is
helpful for seeing that the construction of
{\mathcal{S}}
using cubical spaces must yield the same
results as a more standard argument within simplicial spaces and, in fact, must produce the
standard
\infty
-category of
\infty
-groupoids.

Suppose that
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
was constructed as the base of the universal covariant fibration among

simplicial
types, we can then argue that
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
is also necessarily equivalent to the
base of the universal covariant fibration among cubical spaces. First, observe that the universal
fibration
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{\bullet}\to{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
is covariant when viewed as a map of cubical spaces:
covariance is equivalent to the map
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{\bullet}$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{\mathbb{I}}\to{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{\bullet}\times_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{\mathbb{I}}
being an equivalence and simplicial spaces are a full
subcategory of cubical spaces closed under limits and exponentials. Next, we see that this entails
the existence of a classifying map
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{S}}
and it is routine to calculate that this
map induces an equivalence
\hom\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbb{I}^{n},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\simeq\hom\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbb{I}^{n},{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
since
\mathbb{I}^{n}
is
simplicial. Consequently,
{\mathcal{S}}\simeq{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
. In fact, a nearly identical argument shows that

{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
must in turn agree with the base of a covariant family universal among covariant
families of complete Segal spaces (i.e.,
\infty
-categories) as well—again assuming such a
thing exists.

Consequently, if one assumes the ordinary statement of straightening–unstraightening for the

\infty
-category of
\infty
-groupoids, then the base of the universal covariant family in
cubical spaces must agree with the standard
\infty
-category of
\infty
-groupoids. While not a
satisfactory method of constructing
{\mathcal{S}}
, this does show that our construction must yield the
expected result.

6.1.
The two key lemmas

Before we can prove that
{\mathcal{S}}
is directed univalent, we require a better understanding of when
two maps
\mathbb{I}\to{\mathcal{S}}
are equivalent. In particular, suppose we are given

f,g:\mathbb{I}\to{\mathcal{S}}
. We already know that
{\mathcal{S}}
is univalent as a subtype of
\mathcal{U}_{\boxbslash}

and so
f
and
g
are equal when there is an equivalence
\alpha:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to g\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. Accordingly, it suffices to find conditions to establish that
\alpha\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is an
equivalence for each
i:\mathbb{I}
. Our first result shows that this holds everywhere if it holds at

0
and
1
. In other words, to check that a natural transformation
\alpha
is an equivalence, it
suffices to check that it is an equivalence at each object. We prove a slight generalization of this
result which applies to any
\Delta^{\ell}
rather than just
\Delta^{1}
.

{notation}

We denote
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1,\dots,1,0,\dots 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\Delta^{\ell}
with
k
copies of
1
followed by

\ell-k
copies of
0
by
\bar{k}
.

is invertible for all
k\leq\ell
.

Proof.

We begin by generalizing to apply
Section

3.1.4
. To this end, fix the following global
types:

\displaystyle X=\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{F\,G:\Delta^{\ell}\to{\mathcal{S}}}\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{\alpha:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\delta:\Delta^{\ell}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to F\,\delta\to G\,\delta}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle k:{\mathsf{Nat}}_{\leq\ell}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{isEquiv}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\alpha\,\bar{k}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle Y=\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{F\,G:\Delta^{\ell}\to{\mathcal{S}}}\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{\alpha:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\delta:\Delta^{\ell}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to F\,\delta\to G\,\delta}\textstyle\prod\nolimits_{\delta:\Delta^{\ell}}\mathsf{isEquiv}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\alpha\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\delta$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

It suffices to show that the forgetful map
Y\to X
is an equivalence and so, by

Section

3.1.4
, we must show that for each
n\mathrel{:_{\flat}}{\mathsf{Nat}}
the map

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\mathbb{I}^{n}\to Y$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\mathbb{I}^{n}\to X$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
is an equivalence. For clarity, we write

\Gamma=\mathbb{I}^{n}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}=\mathbb{I}^{n}\times\Delta^{\ell}
in what follows.

We now unfold this slightly. Fix
F,G\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\Gamma^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{S}}
along with

\alpha\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle v,\delta$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\Gamma^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle v,\delta$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to G\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle v,\delta$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and

e\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle v:\Gamma$}\mathclose{}{)}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle k:{\mathsf{Nat}}_{\leq\ell}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{isEquiv}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\alpha\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle v,\bar{k}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. We must show the following:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle v,\delta$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\Gamma^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{isEquiv}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\alpha\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle v,\delta$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}

We can reorient
F,G
as global families
\pi_{F},\pi_{G}:\hbox to39.86pt{\vbox to9.46pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.94446pt\lower-1.94443pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{}{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.94446pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -9.61 0)} \pgfsys@hbox{59}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
. That
both
