factor through
{\mathcal{S}}
implies that both projections are both covariant fibrations
and, therefore, orthogonal to the maps
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\to\mathbb{I}^{m}
for any

m\mathrel{:_{\flat}}{\mathsf{Nat}}
. Note, too, that from this viewpoint,
\alpha
is a map

\tilde{\alpha}:\tilde{F}\to\tilde{G}
over
\Gamma^{\prime}
such that pulling back along

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{id},\bar{k}$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
induces an equivalence. We must show that

\tilde{\alpha}
is an equivalence.

By another application
Section

3.1.4
, to show that
\tilde{\alpha}
is an equivalence
we must show it induces an equivalence

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\mathbb{I}^{m}\to\tilde{F}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\simeq\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\mathbb{I}^{m}\to\tilde{G}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
. By orthogonality, we
note that

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\mathbb{I}^{m}\to\tilde{F}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\simeq\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\tilde{F}\times_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbb{I}^{m}\to\Gamma^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
. Consequently, it suffices to show that the following map is an equivalence:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\tilde{F}\times_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbb{I}^{m}\to\Gamma^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\tilde{G}\times_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbb{I}^{m}\to\Gamma^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}

We may refactor this using the various properties of
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
to obtain the following
equivalent map:

\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{v\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathbb{I}^{m}\to\Gamma}\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{\theta\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathbb{I}^{m}\to\Delta^{\ell}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle v\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\vec{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}},\theta\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\vec{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{v\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathbb{I}^{m}\to\Gamma}\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{\theta\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathbb{I}^{m}\to\Delta^{\ell}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle v\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\vec{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}},\theta\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\vec{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}

Finally,
\theta\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\vec{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is an element of
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\Delta^{\ell}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
and is therefore
equal to
\bar{k}
for some
k
by
Section

3.1.4
. For any
k
, the map is an equivalence
as it is derived from
\alpha
and our conclusion follows.
∎

{remark}

WL (20)
axiomatized their cobar modality to formulate and postulate a special
case of this lemma (their
equivalence axiom
). In our case, no such steps are required as
this result follows from
Section

3.1.4
. This is not to say that the cobar construction
plays no role in our setting: it is used in
Section

4
implicitly as

Shu (19)
uses it to characterize the injective fibrations we use to model types.

To ensure that elements of
{\mathcal{S}}
are indeed groupoids,
A:\mathcal{U}
lands in the subtype
{\mathcal{S}}

only when it is simplicial in addition to being amazingly covariant. Often, it is easiest to do
this by proving that
A
is amazingly covariant and then applying
\boxbslash
to
A
to obtain a
simplicial type. In order for this to be possible, however, we must know that applying
\boxbslash
to an
amazingly covariant type results in an amazingly covariant type. The next lemma proves (a
generalization of) this fact.

Let us note that the canonical maps
\pi:{\mathcal{S}}\to\mathcal{U}
and

\hat{\boxbslash}:\boxbslash\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}
induce a map
\hat{\boxbslash}\pi:\boxbslash{\mathcal{S}}\to\mathcal{U}
. Showing
that
\boxbslash A
is amazingly covariant if
A
is amazingly covariant corresponds to showing that

\hat{\boxbslash}\pi\circ\eta
factors through
{\mathcal{S}}
. We prove this by proving the following
stronger result:

Lemma 6.7 (Simplicial exchange).

\hat{\boxbslash}\pi:\boxbslash{\mathcal{S}}\to\mathcal{U}
factors through
{\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof.

Given that the composite
\hat{\boxbslash}\pi:\boxbslash{\mathcal{S}}\to\mathcal{U}
mentions no free variables, it
suffices by
Lemma

5.11
to show that
\hat{\boxbslash}\pi
is
covariant.

For concision, we write
X=\boxbslash{\mathcal{S}}
and
\tilde{X}
for
\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:X}\hat{\boxbslash}A
. We
must show that the map given by evaluating at
0
induces an equivalence between

\mathbb{I}\to\tilde{X}
and
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbb{I}\to X$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times_{X}\tilde{X}
. Using
Section

3.1.4

along with the observation that these types are all simplicial, it suffices to show that the
following map is an equivalence for all
n\mathrel{:_{\flat}}{\mathsf{Nat}}
:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\Delta^{n}\times\mathbb{I}\to\tilde{X}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\Delta^{n}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbb{I}\to X$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times_{X}\tilde{X}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}

In other words, we must show that
\Delta^{n}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\to\Delta^{n}\times\mathbb{I}
is

globally
orthogonal to
\tilde{X}\to X
.

We now argue that to prove this, it suffices to show that
\tilde{X}\to X
is globally orthogonal
to
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\to\Delta^{k}
for all
k
. Let us assume that this condition holds for the moment and
show that it suffices to establish orthogonality with respect to

\Delta^{n}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\to\Delta^{n}\times\mathbb{I}
. Considering the sequence of maps

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\to\Delta^{n}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\to\Delta^{n}\times\mathbb{I}
, a standard 3-for-2 argument shows
that it suffices to show that
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\to\Delta^{n}\times\mathbb{I}
is orthogonal to

\tilde{X}\to X
.

To this end, notice that
\Delta^{n}\times\mathbb{I}
is a
subtype of
\mathbb{I}^{n+1}
spanned by tuples
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle v_{1},\dots,v_{n},w$}\mathclose{}{)}}
where

v_{1}\geq\dots\geq v_{n}
. Observing that both
\tilde{X}
and
X
are simplicial, it follows that
a map
\Delta^{n}\times\mathbb{I}\to X
or
\Delta^{n}\times\mathbb{I}\to\tilde{X}
is the same as a map
from the following subtype of
\mathbb{I}^{n+1}
:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle v_{1},\dots,v_{n},w$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathrel{{|}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle w\geq v_{1}\geq\dots\geq v_{n}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\lor\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle v_{1}\geq w\geq\dots\geq v_{n}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\lor\dots\lor\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle v_{1}\geq\dots\geq v_{n}\geq w$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}

Let us denote the condition
v_{1}\geq\dots\geq v_{k}\geq w\geq v_{k+1}\geq v_{n}
by
\Phi_{n,k}

with
\Phi_{n,0}
being
w\geq v_{1}\geq\dots\geq v_{n}
. We wish to show that for any element

\tilde{x}\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\tilde{X}
and map
f\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\Delta^{n}\times\mathbb{I}\to X
along
with a path
p\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\pi\,\tilde{x}=f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0,\dots,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}
that there exists a unique
extension of
f
to
\tilde{f}
. We may phrase this as constructing an element of the following type:

\mathsf{isContr}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle t:\Delta^{n}\times\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{\tilde{y}:\tilde{X}}\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{q:\pi\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\tilde{y}$}\mathclose{}{)}}=f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle t$}\mathclose{}{)}}}t=0\to\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{r:\tilde{x}=\tilde{y}}p=\pi\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle r$}\mathclose{}{)}}\bullet q$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}

Since we are therefore constructing a map out of a series of disjunctions (and constructing an
identification of such maps) it suffices to show that this proposition is inhabited for each
subtype
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle v_{1},\dots,v_{n},w$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathrel{{|}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Phi_{n,k_{0}}\land\dots\land\Phi_{n,k_{i}}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
for each
sequence
i
and
k_{0}\leq k_{1}\dots\leq k_{i}
. Calculation reveals that the intersection

\Phi_{n,k_{0}}\land\dots\land\Phi_{n,k_{i}}
is necessarily a sub-simplex of
\Delta^{n}\times\mathbb{I}
. Consequently, unfolding definitions, constructing such a map on this subtype is precisely
the same as showing that
\tilde{X}\to X
is globally orthogonal
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\to\Delta^{k}
for
some
k
, our assumption.

All told then, it suffices to show the following canonical map is an equivalence:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\Delta^{m}\to\tilde{X}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Delta^{m}\to X$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times_{X}\tilde{X}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}

By
Section

3.1.4
, we may “remove the
\boxbslash
” from
X
and
\tilde{X}
and so
this type is equivalent to the following:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\langle}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\Delta^{m}\to\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:{\mathcal{S}}}A$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\rangle}}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\langle}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Delta^{m}\to{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times_{{\mathcal{S}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:{\mathcal{S}}}A$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\rangle}}}

This, finally, is an equivalence because
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:{\mathcal{S}}}A$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}\to{\mathcal{S}}
is
covariant (
Lemma

5.10
).
∎

Corollary 6.8.

{\mathcal{S}}
is closed under coequalizers in
\mathcal{U}_{\boxbslash}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

5.12
,
\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{ACov}}
is closed under coequalizers
\mathsf{Coeq}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}

and so
Lemma

6.7
ensures the
\boxbslash\mathsf{Coeq}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}
lands in
{\mathcal{S}}

as well. By
RSS (20)
, this is the coequalizer in
\mathcal{U}_{\boxbslash}
.
∎

6.2.
{\mathcal{S}}
is directed univalent, Segal, Rezk, and simplicial

We are now able to show that
{\mathcal{S}}
satisfies all the desired properties for a universe of
groupoids. We begin by showing that we have, at last, constructed a directed univalent universe.

First, we note that
Definition

1.1
merely states that there is some isomorphism between two
types. We are already in a position to construct one of these two maps:

Lemma 6.9.

There is a function
\mathsf{mor2fun}
from
\mathbb{I}\to{\mathcal{S}}
to
\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A\,B:{\mathcal{S}}}A\to B
.

Proof.

Given
F:\mathbb{I}\to{\mathcal{S}}
, by
Lemma

5.10
this induces a
covariant family
F_{0}:\mathbb{I}\to\mathcal{U}
. We then define

\mathsf{mor2fun}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle F$}\mathclose{}{)}}\coloneq\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle F_{0}\,0,F_{0}\,1,\mathsf{coe}_{F}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
where the last component is induced by

Lemma

5.4
.
∎

Theorem 6.10 (Directed univalence).

The function
\mathsf{mor2fun}
is an equivalence.

Prior to proving this result, we will construct a putative inverse to
\mathsf{mor2fun}
.

Definition 6.11.

Given
A,B:{\mathcal{S}}
and
f:A\to B
,
\mathsf{Gl}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,B,f$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\mathbb{I}\to{\mathcal{S}}
is

\lambda i.\,\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{b:B}i=0\to f^{-1}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle b$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

\mathsf{Gl}
is the directed version of the glue type from cubical type
theory
(
Coh+, 17
;
Sat, 17
)
and is inspired directly from the construction used in

WL (20)
in their construction of a directed univalent universe.
13
13

13

Not to be
confused with
Artin gluing
from categorical logic.
In our case, we have no need to add it as
a primitive in our setting: this was necessary in (bi)cubical type theory to achieve certain
definitional equalities, but we are pervasively working up to equivalence. We note that

\mathsf{Gl}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f$}\mathclose{}{)}}
factors through
{\mathcal{S}}
by virtue of (2–4,6) of
Lemma

5.12

along
Section

3.1.2
which ensures that
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid\neg j=1$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}=\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle j=0$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.
We also record a few elementary fact about
\mathsf{Gl}
below:

Lemma 6.12.

Given
A,B,f
as above,
\mathsf{Gl}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,B,f$}\mathclose{}{)}}\,0=A
,
\mathsf{Gl}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,B,f$}\mathclose{}{)}}\,1=B
, and

\mathsf{coe}_{\mathsf{Gl}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,B,f$}\mathclose{}{)}}}=f
.

We now return to the proof of
Theorem

6.10
.

Proof of
Theorem

6.10
.

We will prove that
\mathsf{Gl}
forms a quasi-inverse to
\mathsf{mor2fun}
and thereby conclude that

\mathsf{mor2fun}
is an equivalence. We must therefore prove (1)
\mathsf{mor2fun}\circ\mathsf{Gl}=\mathsf{id}
and
(2)
\mathsf{Gl}\circ\mathsf{mor2fun}=\mathsf{id}
. (1) follows from direct calculation and

Lemma

6.12
, so we will detail only (2).

Suppose we are given
F:\mathbb{I}\to{\mathcal{S}}
. We must show that
F=\mathsf{Gl}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{mor2fun}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle F$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
or
equivalently, using the fact that
{\mathcal{S}}
is univalent, that there is an equivalence

\alpha:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}\simeq\mathsf{Gl}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{mor2fun}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle F$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\,i
. To prove this, we
will begin by constructing
\alpha
and then use
Lemma

6.6
to reduce to checking
that
\alpha
is an equivalence at
0
and
1
. It is helpful to do this in stages and so we
begin by supposing
i:\mathbb{I}
and
z:F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and define
\alpha
as follows for some
X
and

Y
to be determined:

\alpha\,i\,z=\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle X:F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}},Y:i=0\to\mathsf{coe}_{F}^{-1}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle X$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

We will construct
X
and
Y
separately.

We can substantiate
X
immediately:
\mathsf{coe}_{F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-\lor i$}\mathclose{}{)}}}:F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and so we
choose
X\coloneq F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-\lor i$}\mathclose{}{)}}\,z
. This refines the type of
Y
to
i=0\to\mathsf{coe}_{F}^{-1}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{coe}_{F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-\lor i$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\,z$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. Assume
\phi:i=0
so that it suffices to define

Y.1:F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and
Y.2:\mathsf{coe}_{F}\,Y.1=\mathsf{coe}_{F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-\lor i$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\,z
. Using
\phi
, we may suppose
that
z:F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and that the type of
Y.2
is
\mathsf{coe}_{F}\,Y.1=\mathsf{coe}_{F}\,z

(since
0\lor-=\mathsf{id}
). After this,
Y.1\coloneq z
and
Y.2\coloneq{\mathsf{refl}}
suffices.

Finally, it is now straightforward to check that
\alpha\,0
and
\alpha\,1
are equivalences
using
Lemma

6.12
.
∎

The proof that
{\mathcal{S}}
is Segal is very similar to the proof of directed univalence, though not
quite a consequence of it. Since the proof is similar to
Theorem

6.10
, we provide only a
sketch.

Lemma 6.13.

{\mathcal{S}}
is Segal.

Proof sketch.

We must show that
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Delta^{2}\to{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Lambda^{2}_{1}\to{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is an
equivalence. We begin by noting that the codomain can be rewritten with
Theorem

6.10
as

T=\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A\,B\,C:{\mathcal{S}}}A\to B\times B\to C
. We only need to show that the
forgetful map from
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Delta^{2}\to{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to T
is an equivalence.

This argument proceeds along the same lines as
Theorem

6.10
where we replace

\mathbb{I}
with
\Delta^{2}
: we introduce a variant of
\mathsf{Gl}
which glues together three spaces along
two maps and show that this procedure induces a quasi-inverse to the forgetful map

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Delta^{2}\to{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to T
. It is here that we require
Lemma

6.6
with

\ell=2
rather than
\ell=1
.
∎

Corollary 6.14.

Composition of the morphisms in
{\mathcal{S}}
is realized by ordinary function composition.

In particular, an invertible morphism corresponds via
Theorem

6.10
to an
equivalence. Combining this with ordinary univalence, we obtain:

Corollary 6.15.

{\mathcal{S}}
is Rezk.

Our final result is that
{\mathcal{S}}
lands in the subuniverse of simplicial types.

Lemma 6.16.

{\mathcal{S}}
is simplicial.

Proof.

By
RSS (20, Lemma 1.20)
, it suffices to show that
\eta:{\mathcal{S}}\to\boxbslash{\mathcal{S}}
has a
retraction. By univalence, the composite of
\eta:\mathcal{U}_{\boxbslash}\to\boxbslash\mathcal{U}_{\boxbslash}
followed by

\hat{\boxbslash}:\boxbslash\mathcal{U}_{\boxbslash}\to\mathcal{U}_{\boxbslash}
is the identity and so it suffices to show that
both these maps restrict to
{\mathcal{S}}
. That is, it suffices to show that

\hat{\boxbslash}\circ\pi:\boxbslash{\mathcal{S}}\to\mathcal{U}
factors through
{\mathcal{S}}
. This is an immediate
consequence of
Lemma

6.7
.
∎

We conclude by noting a few of the categorical properties
{\mathcal{S}}
enjoys:

Lemma 6.17.

{\mathcal{S}}
is finitely complete and finitely cocomplete and satisfies
descent

(
Rij, 18
, Chapter
2)
.

Proof Sketch.

Finite completeness and cocompleteness are an immediate consequence of

Lemmas

6.4
and
6.8
along with
Theorem

6.10
which implies that a e.g.,
categorical limit in
{\mathcal{S}}
is an ordinary
HoTT
limit of groupoids. To prove the descent
properties, we must show that various limits and colimits commute appropriately. However, by

Theorem

6.10
once more, this is an immediate consequence of the fact that limits and
colimits in
HoTT
enjoy descent
(
Rij, 18
)
.
∎

7.
Consequences of a directed univalent universe

We now reap the rewards of our efforts in constructing
{\mathcal{S}}
and give a brief tour of the
consequences of this type. We show how directed univalence may be used to prove free theorems and
substantiate the structure homomorphism principle. We also use it to construct various
foundational example categories and lay the groundwork for the development of
higher algebra

within
TT
\boxbslash
.

7.1.
Free theorems from naturality

Directed univalence allows us to make a precise link between familiar parametricity
arguments
(
Wad, 89
)
with the categorical naturality arguments that helped motivate
them. In particular, directed univalence implies that a function

\alpha:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A:{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to G\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is natural:

Lemma 7.1.

If
F_{0},F_{1}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}
and
\alpha:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A:{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to F_{0}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to F_{1}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
then

\alpha\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle B$}\mathclose{}{)}}\circ F_{0}(f)=F_{1}(f)\circ\alpha\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
for any
f:A\to B
.

Proof.

Fix
A,B:{\mathcal{S}}
along with
f:A\to B
and denote the corresponding morphism

G:\mathbb{I}\to{\mathcal{S}}
. Note that
\alpha\circ G
is then a function

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to F_{0}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to F_{1}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. Applying
Theorem

6.10
once more, we note
that
\alpha\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle G\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}:F_{0}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to F_{1}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is a morphism in
{\mathcal{S}}
for every

i
. Accordingly,
\alpha\circ G
is equivalent to some
s:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i\,j:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to H\,i\,j
for
some
H
where
H\,i\,0=F_{0}\,i
and
H\,i\,1=F\,i
. We visualize
H
as:

F_{0}\,0
F_{1}\,0
\alpha\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle G\,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}
F_{0}\,1
F_{1}\,1
F_{0}
\alpha\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle G\,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}
F_{1}

This commuting square is equivalently an equality between the composites
F_{1}
and

\alpha\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle G\,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and
\alpha\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle G\,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and
F_{0}
. The conclusion then follows from

Corollary

6.14
.
∎

\polyid

Proof.

Fix
A:{\mathcal{S}}
and suppose we are given
a:A
. Applying
Lemma

7.1
to
f

and
\lambda\_.\,a
, we conclude that
f\,A\,\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a\,\star$}\mathclose{}{)}}=a\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f\,\mathbf{1}\,\star$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. Since

f\,\mathbf{1}\,\star=\star
by the
\eta
principle of
\mathbf{1}
,
f=\lambda A\,a.\,a
.
∎

Nothing limits us to considering only operations
{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}
. The same techniques scale to
multi-argument operations such as
{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}
or even mixed-variance
operations such as
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\times{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}
:

Lemma 7.2.

If
\alpha:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A\,B:{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to A\times B\to A
then
\alpha=\pi_{1}
.

Lemma 7.3.

If
A,B\mathrel{:_{\flat}}{\mathcal{S}}
and

\alpha:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle C\mathrel{:_{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to A^{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid C$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}}\to B^{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid C$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}}
then

\alpha=\lambda\_g.f\circ g
for some
f:A\to B
.

This methodology highlights the
limitations
of naturality as a facsimile for parametricity:
for operations whose parameters are not used strictly co- or contravariantly, directed univalence
does not provide any free theorems. We leave it to future work to consider alternative universes of
correspondences
(
AF, 20
)
and what parametricity arguments they might provide.

7.2.
Full subcategories of
{\mathcal{S}}

A large number of important categories can be described as a
full subcategories
of
{\mathcal{S}}
.
To do this, we must first show how to obtain full subcategories inside of
TT
\boxbslash
. Recall that a full
subcategory of
C_{0}
of a category
C\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}
is a category
C_{0}
where objects
are a subset of those in
C
but the morphisms and all the higher cells agree. In other words, a
full subcategory is described by a predicate
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid C$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{HProp}_{\boxbslash}
which picks out those
objects which land in
C_{0}
.

Definition 7.4.

Given
\phi\mathrel{:_{\sharp}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid C$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{HProp}_{\boxbslash}
, the resulting full subcategory

C_{\phi}
is
\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{c:C}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp\mid\phi\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle c^{\eta}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
.
14
14

14

In practice,
\phi
will be

\flat
-annotated.

Here we for the first time have occasion to explicitly use the right adjoint
\sharp
to
\flat
. Let us
note that
C_{\phi}
is a category because (1) categories are closed under dependent sums and (2)

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp\mid\phi\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle c^{\eta}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
is a groupoid. Furthermore, we can prove that
C_{\phi}
is
actually a full subcategory:

Lemma 7.5.

Given
C
and
\phi
as above, if
a,b:C_{\phi}
then

\hom_{C_{\phi}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}\simeq\hom_{C}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\pi_{1}\,a,\pi_{1}\,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

Proof.

Unfolding definitions, it suffices to show the following two propositions are equivalent for all

f:\mathbb{I}\to C
:

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp\mid\phi\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f^{\eta}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp\mid\phi\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f^{\eta}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp\mid\phi\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f^{\eta}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i^{\eta}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}

Since
\flat\dashv\sharp
, we may use
Lemma

3.8
to replace the second proposition
with

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp\mid\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\phi\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f^{\eta}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
. Finally,

Section

3.1.4
tells us that
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
is equivalent to
{\mathsf{Bool}}
and we may
replace
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\phi\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f^{\eta}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
with

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle b:{\mathsf{Bool}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\phi\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f^{\eta}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{if}\ b\ \mathsf{then}\ 0\ \mathsf{else}\ 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and
conclusion follows.
∎

Lemma 7.6.

Given a category
C\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}
and
\phi\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid C$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{HProp}_{\boxbslash}

then
a\mathrel{:_{\flat}}C
is an element of
C_{\phi}
if and only if
\phi\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}
holds.

By choosing different predicates on
{\mathcal{S}}
we obtain a number of familiar
categories. For instance:

Definition 7.7.

The category of
n
-truncated groupoids
{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq n}
is given by

{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{hasHLevel}\,(n+2)}
.
15
15

15

The correction
+2
ensures that
{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq n}

comports with the standard indexing in homotopy theory which begins at
-2
, not
0
.
In
particular, the category of
propositions
is given by
{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq-1}
, and the category of

sets
is given by
{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}
.

Definition 7.8.

The category of finite sets
\mathcal{F}
is given by
{\mathcal{S}}_{\phi}
where

\phi\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle X$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{n:{\mathsf{Nat}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle X={\mathsf{Nat}}_{\leq n}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

Note that
\mathcal{F}
is quite different than
\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:{\mathcal{S}}}\exists n.\,{\mathsf{Nat}}_{\leq n}=A
, which
has only invertible morphisms. The definition of
C_{\phi}
is necessary to ensure that
\phi
is
applied only to the objects of
C
, not its higher cells.

Lemma

7.5
implies that these examples inherit directed univalence from

{\mathcal{S}}
, the first instance of the
structure homomorphism principle
(SHP)

(
WL, 20
)
: homomorphisms in structured types coincide with their standard
analytic formulations and, consequently, all terms and types are functorial for these analytic
morphisms. For instance, a morphism in
\mathcal{F}
corresponds to an ordinary function and,
consequently, a family
F:\mathcal{F}\to{\mathcal{S}}
has an action
F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to F\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle B$}\mathclose{}{)}}
for any
ordinary function
A\to B
.

7.3.
The structure homomorphism principle

Not only full subcategories of
{\mathcal{S}}
enjoy SHP, in this section we survey other
categories which satisfy it as well. As a prototypical example, we consider pointed spaces,

{\mathcal{S}}_{*}=\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:{\mathcal{S}}}A
:

Lemma 7.9.

Homomorphisms
\hom_{{\mathcal{S}}_{*}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,a$}\mathclose{}{)}},\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle B,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
are equal to pointed functions

\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{f:A\to B}f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}=b
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

6.2
, the projection map
{\mathcal{S}}_{*}\to{\mathcal{S}}
is
covariant, giving, for any pair of pointed spaces
(A,a_{0})
and
(B,b_{0})
, an equivalence between
homomorphisms from
a_{0}
to
b_{0}
lying over a homomorphism
f:A\to B
and identifications

f(a_{0})=b_{0}
.
∎

This same methodology can be applied to more general algebraic structures to yield categories of
e.g., monoids, groups, rings, etc. which we conjecture all enjoy SHP. Rather than dealing with this
generality, we will focus on monoids to complete the example given in
Section

1
. We
recall the type of monoids:

\mathsf{Monoid}=\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}}\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{\epsilon:A}\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{\cdot:A\times A\to A}\mathsf{isAssociative}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\cdot$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\mathsf{isUnit}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\cdot,\epsilon$}\mathclose{}{)}}

By repeated application of the closure of categories under dependent sums, functions, and
equalities, we already conclude that
\mathsf{Monoid}
is a category. Moreover, we can
characterize its homomorphisms.

Lemma 7.10.

A homomorphism

\hom\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,\epsilon_{A},\cdot_{A},\alpha_{A},\mu_{A}$}\mathclose{}{)}},\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle B,\epsilon_{B},\cdot_{B},\alpha_{B},\mu_{B}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is
precisely a standard monoid homomorphism e.g. a function
A\to B
commuting with multiplication
and the unit.

Proof.

To show that the type
\mathsf{Monoid}
is a category, we proceed in several steps. Let us denote by
{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0,*}
the category of pointed sets
\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}}A
. We obtain define the type
\mathsf{Mag}\coloneq\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0,*}}{A\times A\to A}
of
magmas
via the pullback, showing that it is a category:

\mathsf{Mag}
{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}^{\mathbb{I}}
{\mathsf{Unit}}
{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle s,t$}\mathclose{}{)}}
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A\times A,A$}\mathclose{}{)}}

We get a natural projection from the type
\mathsf{Mag}_{*}
of
pointed magmas
which is also a covariant fibration as evinced by the following diagram:

\mathsf{Mag}_{*}
{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0,*}
{\mathcal{S}}_{*}
\mathsf{Mag}
{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}
{\mathcal{S}}

The type
\mathsf{Monoid}
is then a subtype of
\mathsf{Mag}_{*}
, so
\mathsf{Monoid}\simeq\mathsf{Mag}\times_{\mathsf{Prop}}{\mathsf{Unit}}
, and hence
\mathsf{Monoid}
is a category.

We compute the (free) morphisms in
\mathsf{Monoid}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}\to\mathsf{Monoid}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbb{I}\to\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}}\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{\varepsilon_{A}:A}\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{\mu_{A}:A\times A\to A}\mathsf{isAssociative}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu_{A}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\mathsf{isUnit}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu_{A},\epsilon_{A}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle E:\mathbb{I}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\epsilon:\textstyle\prod\nolimits_{i:\mathbb{I}}E(i)$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu:\textstyle\prod\nolimits_{i:\mathbb{I}}E(i)\times E(i)\to E(i)$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{isAssociative}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu(i)$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\mathsf{isUnit}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu(i),\epsilon(i)$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{thm:space:dua}}}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle E:\mathbb{I}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\epsilon:\textstyle\prod\nolimits_{i:\mathbb{I}}E(i)$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu:\textstyle\prod\nolimits_{i:\mathbb{I}}\hom_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle E(i)\times E(i),E(i)$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{isAssociative}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu(i)$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\mathsf{isUnit}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu(i),\epsilon(i)$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle E:\mathbb{I}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\epsilon:\textstyle\prod\nolimits_{i:\mathbb{I}}E(i)$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu:\textstyle\prod\nolimits_{i,j:\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}}\hom_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle E(i)\times E(i),E(i)$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\textstyle\prod\nolimits_{i:\mathbb{I}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{isAssociative}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\lambda j.\mu(i,j)$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\mathsf{isUnit}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\lambda j.\mu(i,j),\epsilon(i)$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{thm:space:dua}}\\
\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lem:consequences:pointed-spaces}}\end{subarray}}}{{\simeq}}\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}A,B:{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}\\
f:A\to B\end{subarray}}\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\epsilon_{A}:A\\
\epsilon_{B}:B\end{subarray}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f(a)=b$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu_{A}:A\times A\to A\\
\mu_{B}:B\times B\to B\end{subarray}}f\circ\mu_{A}=\mu_{B}\circ(f\times f)

\displaystyle\times\mathsf{isAssociative}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu_{A}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\mathsf{isAssociative}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mu_{B}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\mathsf{isUnit}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\varepsilon_{A}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\mathsf{isUnit}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\varepsilon_{B}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

In the final step, we have additionally used the characterization of squares
\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}\to A
in a category
A
as (homotopy) commutative squares of morphisms. For illustration, we can represent the square
\mu
in terms of the family
E
as follows:
A
B
A\times A
B\times B
E
E\times E
\mu(0,-)
\mu(1,-)

Overall, the claim follows by taking fibers of
\mathsf{Monoid}^{\mathbb{I}}\to\mathsf{Monoid}\times\mathsf{Monoid}
, using the above characterization.

∎

Substituting
Lemma

7.10
within
Lemma

7.1
, we obtain
the promised result:

\monoidnat

To complete our goal of proving
\mathsf{sum}
natural automatically, it remains only to define

\mathsf{List}
as an endomap of monoids where
\mathsf{List}\,A
has pointwise
multiplication. Remarkably, this is straightforward consequence of our results. One need only write
down the definition of this monoid in the ordinary way and conclude that it lifts to a functor
because the carrier (
\mathsf{List}=\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{n:{\mathsf{Nat}}}-^{n}
) is already known to be a functor

{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}
using the closure under
\Sigma
and
{\mathsf{Nat}}
; no
special argument is required.

We can also apply directed univalence to non-algebraic structures using our ability to define

n
-presheaf categories
\mathrm{PSh}_{n}(C)=\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid C$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq n}
. We consider the
representative example of partial orders, which we isolate as a full subcategory of a presheaf
category. In particular, we begin with the category of reflexive graphs:

{\mathsf{RGraph}}=\mathrm{PSh}_{0}(\Delta_{\leq 1})
where
\Delta_{\leq 1}
is the “walking fork” given by the
pushout
\Delta^{2}\mathrel{\textstyle\sqcup_{\mathbb{I}}}\Delta^{2}
adjoining a pair of retractions
\partial_{0},\partial_{1}

to a single arrow
r:1\to 0
. While we have not ensured
\Delta_{\leq 1}
is a category, this does
not matter as
{\mathsf{RGraph}}
is a category regardless.

We use directed univalence to characterize this category’s
objects
as well as its higher
structure:

Lemma 7.11.

The category
{\mathsf{RGraph}}
is equivalent to

\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{G_{0}:{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}}\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{G_{1}:G_{0}\times G_{0}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}}\textstyle\prod\nolimits_{x:G_{0}}G_{1}(x,x).

Proof.

Using the universal property of a pushout,

{\mathsf{RGraph}}={\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}^{\Delta^{2}}\times_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathbb{I}}}{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}^{\Delta^{2}}
and so
repeated application of
Theorems

6.10
and
6.13
proves

{\mathsf{RGraph}}=\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{G_{0}\,G_{1}:{\mathcal{S}}_{\leq 0}}\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{s\,t:G_{1}\to G_{0}}\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{r:G_{0}\to G_{1}}sr=st

and the conclusion now follows from a standard argument.
∎

We isolate
{\mathsf{Pos}}\subseteq{\mathsf{RGraph}}
as a full subcategory spanned by objects where
G_{1}
is a
partial order:

Definition 7.12.

{\mathsf{Pos}}={\mathsf{RGraph}}_{\phi}
where

\phi\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle G$}\mathclose{}{)}}\coloneq\mathsf{isASym}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle G_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\mathsf{isTrans}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle G_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\textstyle\prod\nolimits_{x,y:G_{0}}\mathsf{isHProp}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle G_{1}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

Lemma

7.5
now proves that homomorphisms in
{\mathsf{Pos}}
are precisely monotone maps:

Lemma 7.13.

If
P,Q:{\mathsf{Pos}}
then

\hom_{{\mathsf{Pos}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle P,Q$}\mathclose{}{)}}\simeq\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{f:P_{0}\to Q_{0}}{\prod_{x,y:P_{0}}P_{1}(x,y)\to Q_{1}(fx,fy)}
.

Finally, for the next subsection we isolate a category which is foundational to
\infty
-category
theory: the simplex category
\mathbbl{\Delta}
is the full subcategory
{\mathsf{Pos}}_{\phi}
where

\phi\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle P$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{n:{\mathsf{Nat}}}P=\Delta^{n}
.

7.4.
First steps in synthetic higher algebra

As homotopy (type) theorists like to quip: homotopy types are modern sets. Higher algebra seeks to
take this slogan a step further by studying groups, rings, modules, etc. in a world where homotopy
types have replaced sets. While higher algebra has numerous applications to algebraic topology,
algebraic K-theory, and algebraic geometry, it is also a notoriously technical: even the simplest
higher algebraic structure must account for an infinite tower of coherences for each imposed
equation. For our final application of
{\mathcal{S}}
, we initiate the study of
higher
algebra

(
Lur, 17
;
Gep, 20
)
in
TT
\boxbslash
by defining some of the central objects of
study. We begin by defining the category of (homotopy-coherent and untruncated) monoids following

Seg (74)
.

Definition 7.14.

The
category of coherent monoids

\mathsf{Monoid}_{\infty}
as the full subcategory of
\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta)

carved out by the following predicate (the Segal condition):

\displaystyle\phi\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle X\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid\Delta$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to{\mathcal{S}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}=

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{isContr}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle(X(\Delta^{0}))$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\textstyle\prod\nolimits_{n:{\mathsf{Nat}}_{\geq 1}}\mathsf{isEquiv}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle X\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\iota_{k}$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{k<n}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}:X\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Delta^{n}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to X\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Delta^{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{n}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

In the above,
\iota_{k}:\Delta^{1}\to\Delta^{n}
is
\lambda i.\,\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle 1,\dots,1,i,0,\dots$}\mathclose{}{)}}

picking out
k
copies of
1
.

In other words, a coherent monoid is a functor
X:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid\Delta$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to{\mathcal{S}}
such that

X\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Delta^{n}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is the
n
-fold product of
X\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Delta^{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. While somewhat indirect, these
conditions encode all the necessary structure e.g., the
unit
is given by the center of contraction
\varepsilon_{X}:X(\Delta^{0})
, and
multiplication
is
given by the composite map
\mu_{X}:X(\Delta^{1})^{2}\simeq X(\Delta^{2})\to X(\Delta^{1})
.

As a small example of manipulating this definition, we prove the following:

Lemma 7.15.

The functor
\mathsf{Monoid}_{\infty}\to{\mathcal{S}}
induced by evaluation at
\Delta^{1}
reflects
isomorphisms.

Proof.

Given
f:X\to Y
, by
RS (17)
and
Lemma

7.5
, it
suffices to show that if
f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Delta^{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is an isomorphism so is
f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Delta^{n}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
for any
n
. By
the Segal condition and naturality,
f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Delta^{n}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is equivalent to

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Delta^{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{i\leq n}
which is invertible if
f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Delta^{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is an
isomorphism.
∎

Once again, directed univalence yields that the morphisms in
\mathsf{Monoid}_{\infty}
preserve the relevant structure.

Proposition 7.16.

Let
X,Y\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathsf{Monoid}_{\infty}
. Then
\hom_{\mathsf{Monoid}_{\infty}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle X,Y$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is equivalent to the type of natural transformations
X\to Y
. In particular, for
F\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\hom_{\mathsf{Monoid}_{\infty}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle X,Y$}\mathclose{}{)}}
we have homotopies
F(\Delta^{0})(\epsilon_{X})=\epsilon_{Y}
in
Y(\Delta^{0})
, and in the category of spaces
{\mathcal{S}}
the following homotopy-commutative diagram:

X(\Delta^{1})^{2}\simeq X(\Delta^{2})
X(\Delta^{1})
\mu_{X}
Y(\Delta^{2})\simeq Y(\Delta^{1})^{2}
Y(\Delta^{1})
F(\Delta^{2})
\mu_{Y}
F(\Delta^{1})

Proof.

Analogously to
RS, 17
, Proposition 6.6
,
F
is a natural transformation. Then preservation of the unit follows by contractibility, and preservation of multiplication follows by directed univalence and naturality.
∎

We can also define the category of coherent
groups
:

Definition 7.17.

The
category of coherent groups

\mathsf{Grp}_{\infty}
is the full subcategory of

\mathsf{Monoid}_{\infty}
carved out by the predicate

\phi{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle X\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathsf{Monoid}_{\infty}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}=\mathsf{isEquiv}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\lambda x\,y.\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,\mu\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}:X\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Delta^{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{2}\to X\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Delta^{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{2}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

These concepts and many others can be unified through the formalism of (
\infty
-)operads but we
leave it to future work to develop this apparatus in
TT
\boxbslash
. An application of such a
formalism would be the ability to develop higher algebra not just in
{\mathcal{S}}
, but in

spectra
, another fundamental category in modern homotopy theory. We conclude this section by
constructing this category.

Suppose
C
is a pointed category with pullbacks, i.e.,
C
has pullbacks and comes with an element

0:C
which is simultaneously initial terminal and initial. Within
C
, we define the
loop
functor

\Omega:C\to C
by
\Omega\coloneq\lambda x.0\times_{x}0
. We have already
encountered such a pointed category:
{\mathcal{S}}_{*}
.

Definition 7.18.

The
category of spectra

\mathsf{Sp}
is defined as

\mathop{\mathsf{lim}}_{n:{\mathsf{Nat}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Omega}}{{\to}}{\mathcal{S}}_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Omega}}{{\to}}{\mathcal{S}}_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Omega}}{{\to}}{\mathcal{S}}_{*}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

Here
\lim
refers to the ordinary definition of a limit from
HoTT
and we note that as the limit
of categories,
\mathsf{Sp}
is itself automatically a category. Using directed univalence, we can easily
show that objects of
\mathsf{Sp}
are infinite deloopings of a groupoid as
expected
(
Shu, 13
;
vDoo, 18
)
.

8.
Conclusions and related work

We have introduced
TT
\boxbslash
, an enhancement of simplicial type theory featuring modalities and a
relaxed interval type. We have used
TT
\boxbslash
as a framework to construct a directed univalent universe
of groupoids
{\mathcal{S}}
which we have further proven to be a well-behaved category. Finally, we have
used
{\mathcal{S}}
as a jumping off point to construct numerous examples of categories and categorical
reasoning in
TT
\boxbslash
relevant both to
\infty
-category theory and mechanized verification. In order
to do so, we have shown how our same modal operators can be used to e.g., construct full
subcategories.

8.1.
Related work

While directed type theory generally and simplicial type theory specifically are relatively new
areas, there is already substantial work exploring the impact of a “type theory where types are
categories.” Much of this work focuses on either constructing such type theories
(
LH, 11
;
War, 13
;
Nuy, 15
;
RS, 17
;
Nor, 18
;
Kav, 19
;
Nuy, 20
;
ANvdW, 23
;
NA, 24
;
Neu, 25
)

or studying “formal” category theory within them
(
RS, 17
;
Wei24b,
;
Wei24a,
;
BW, 23
;
Mar, 24
;
Mar, 25
)

i.e., statements which do not use particular closed non-trivial categories but instead quantify
over arbitrary categories. This is distinct from our focus, which has been to combine essentially
off-the-shelf type theories
(
RS, 17
;
Gra+, 21
)
and to use this combination
to prove facts about the concrete type
{\mathcal{S}}
and types derived thereof. Closely related to this
is the work by Cavallo, Riehl, and Sattler
(
Rie, 18
)
and
WL (20)
, who both
study directed univalence, in respectively simplicial and
bicubical type theory
(
BCTT
).

Alternative constructions of
{\mathcal{S}}

Cavallo, Riehl, and Sattler give an alternative construction of
{\mathcal{S}}
in the intended model of

STT
, similar to the classical proof due to
Cis (19)
. They have argued externally
that this subuniverse satisfies directed univalence and a version of

Lemma

6.2
. However, their work is strictly external and does not
consider how one might integrate
{\mathcal{S}}
within
STT
. Given that both our universe and theirs
satisfy
Lemma

6.2
, they are weakly equivalent and so our results
further show that their universe is e.g., a finitely (co)complete category and closed under various
connectives.

Bicubical type theory

Most closely related to our work is the paper of
WL (20)
. Here,

WL
consider a variant of
STT
based on two layers of cubical type theory and
construct a directed-univalent universe in this setting. Their system,
BCTT
, uses two distinct
interval types: one to account for homotopy type theory and a further layer for the directed
interval. Bicubical type theory is therefore to
TT
\boxbslash
as cubical type theory is to
HoTT
. Moreover,
the approach used by op. cit. to construct their universe directly inspired our own approach. In
particular, the definition of amazing covariance and our directed glue type are derived from closely
related constructions in
BCTT
. Moreover, our
directed homomorphism principle
is
elaborating on an idea proposed by
WL (20)
.

The two systems,
TT
\boxbslash
and
BCTT
, differ in a number of ways. Most importantly, it is conjectured
that
BCTT
can be formally presented
16
16

16

WL (20)
do not give a definition of

BCTT
but instead describe the intended model for any such situation. Their model is, however,
constructive and so it is conjectured that such a definition would satisfy canonicity.
so as to
enjoy canonicity and normalization. On the other hand,
TT
\boxbslash
certainly does not satisfy canonicity.
Thus,
BCTT
is likely better suited for “programming” with directed univalence. However,

BCTT
’s categories and groupoids are not expected to be adequate for ordinary
\infty
-categories
or
\infty
-groupoids and so it is not obvious that it can be used for developing synthetic

\infty
-category theory.

More fundamentally, while they also work within an internal language and we draw on their overall
strategy in
Section

6
, theirs is the internal
extensional
type theory of

\mathrm{PSh}(\square_{\mathsf{undirected}}\times\square_{\mathsf{directed}})
and so they must not only
construct
{\mathcal{S}}
but also the model of base
HoTT
around it. This substantially complicates some
of their constructions; their versions of e.g., covariance,
\mathsf{Gl}
and so on include details that
are automatically handled when working pervasively with
HoTT
. This model falsifies

Section

3.1.4
and so they must introduce an additional set of axioms (the cobar modality)
work around this. We believe both approaches to directed type theory warrant further consideration
to (1) study our results on top of base cubical type theory rather than
HoTT
and (2) to translate
our new results to their setting. In particular, op. cit. proves only that
{\mathcal{S}}
is directed
univalent and does not prove e.g.
Lemma

6.13
but we believe our proof, along with those
results in
Section

7
, can be translated.

Other closely related type theories

While not about directed type theory,
MR (23)
also consider a
HoTT
for simplicial
spaces. We drew inspiration for some of our axioms (e.g.
Section

3.1.4
) from them and
expect their other principles will prove useful to
STT
. Furthermore,
CCH (23)

formulated a version of
Section

3.1.4
to study synthetic algebraic geometry which led us to its
inclusion in
TT
\boxbslash
. Finally,
Ril (24)
presents a type theory with a single amazing right
adjoint whose syntax is well-adapted for this situation. We hope that op. cit. can be generalized
for
TT
\boxbslash
to yield more usable syntax.

8.2.
Future work

We isolate three key directions for future work. First, we wish to extend the experimental proof
assistant
Rzk

(
Kud, 23
)
with the minimum level of modal reasoning (e.g., at least

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
,
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sharp\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
and
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
) to properly axiomatize and work with

{\mathcal{S}}
as constructed in this paper. We hope to then use this to mechanize

Section

7
. Related to this, we hope to give a constructive model of
TT
\boxbslash
to give a
computational justification of our axioms. We expect this to contribute to a version of
TT
\boxbslash
with
canonicity and normalization
(
Aag+, 22
;
Gra, 22
)
.

Second, in forthcoming work
(
GWB25a,
)
we have generalized our construction of
{\mathcal{S}}
to construct the category of (small)
categories
{\mathsf{Cat}}
and proven that it is suitably directed univalent
(
CN, 22
)
. While
modalities were required to construct
{\mathcal{S}}
, they are required to
state
the properties
of
{\mathsf{Cat}}
; directed univalence becomes

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\mathbb{I}\to{\mathsf{Cat}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\simeq\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\langle}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\flat\mid\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{A:{\mathsf{Cat}}}\textstyle\sum\nolimits_{B:{\mathsf{Cat}}}A\to B$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\rangle}}}
because
homomorphisms from
A
to
B
must be the
groupoid
of the category of functors
A\to B
, not
the category. Aside from this, our results scale to this more general setting.

Finally, while we discussed presheaf categories in
Section

7
, we avoided describing
the Yoneda embedding
C\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathrm{op}\mid C$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to{\mathcal{S}}
. While it is possible to construct this
operation, it requires one additional modality (the twisted arrow construction) and, for reasons of
space, we have regretfully chosen to omit it in the present work. In subsequent
work
(
GWB25b,
)
17
17

17

The chronology of this work is somewhat confused. The
construction of
{\mathcal{S}}
was completed prior to the cited work on the Yoneda embedding. However,
publication timing has meant that this second paper was published first.
we have detailed this
additional modality along with the resulting definition of the Yoneda embedding. Using this in
conjunction with our work on full subcategories, we are able to prove various important results
e.g. that
{\mathcal{S}}
is cocomplete.

In additional forthcoming work
(
GWB, 25
)
we are discussing cocompleteness and show the category of spectra to be stable.
